Braid Arrangement Bimonoids and the Toric Variety of the Permutohedron
William Norledge

Abstract
. We show that the toric variety of the permutohedron (=permutohedral space) has the structure of a cocommutative bimonoid in species, with multiplication/comultiplication given by embedding/projecting-onto boundary divisors. In terms of Losev-Manin’s description of permutohedral space as a moduli space, multiplication is concatenation of strings of Riemann spheres and comultiplication is forgetting marked points. In this way, the bimonoid structure is an analog of the cyclic operad structure on the moduli space of genus zero marked curves. Covariant/contravariant data on permutohedral space is endowed with the structure of cocommutative/commutative bimonoids by pushing-forward/pulling-back data along the (co)multiplication. Many well-known combinatorial objects index data on permutohedral space. Moreover, combinatorial objects often have the structure of bimonoids, with multiplication/comultiplication given by merging/restricting objects in some way. We prove (for a selection of cases) that the bimonoid structure enjoyed by these indexing combinatorial objects coincides with that induced by the bimonoid structure of permutohedral space. Thus, permutohedral space may be viewed as a fundamental underlying object which geometrically interprets many combinatorial Hopf algebras. Aguiar-Mahajan have shown that classical combinatorial Hopf theory is based on the braid hyperplane arrangement in a crucial way. This paper aims to similarly establish permutohedral space as a central object, providing an even more unified perspective. The main motivation for this work concerns Feynman amplitudes in the Schwinger parametrization, which become integrals over permutohedral space if one blows-up everything in the resolution of singularities. Then the Hopf algebra structure of Feynman graphs, first appearing in the work of Connes-Kreimer, coincides with that induced by the bimonoid structure of permutohedral space.



Introduction

In enumerative combinatorics, the notion of generating function may be categorified as follows. One picks a certain type (‘species’) of combinatorial object
p
, for example
\text{p}=\text{binary trees}
. One decides what are the points of these combinatorial objects, for example
\text{points}=\text{vertices}
of binary trees. Then, for
I
a finite set, form the set
\text{p}[I]
of all
I
-labeled combinatorial objects of type
p
, where by
I
-labeling we mean an (often bijective) function on
I
into the chosen points. The associated generating function just records the cardinalities of the sets
\text{p}[I]
, however there is more structure. For each bijection
\sigma:J\to I
we have a relabeling bijection
\text{p}[\sigma]:\text{p}[I]\to\text{p}[J]
, which precomposes
I
-labelings
with
\sigma
to produce
J
-labelings. Thus, we actually have a presheaf on the category
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
of finite sets and bijections

\text{p}:({\textsf{finSet}^{\times}})^{\text{op}}\to\textsf{Set},\qquad I\mapsto\text{p}[I],\quad\sigma\mapsto\text{p}[\sigma].

In general, for
C
a category, we refer to
C
-valued presheaves on
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
as
C
-valued
Joyal species
, going back to
[
Joy81
]
,
[
Joy86
]
.

Instead of the discrete structures of combinatorics, one can instead consider continuous spaces, for example in species whose components
\text{p}[I]
are configuration spaces of points, or moduli spaces parametrizing spaces with marked points. In this context, species are more often called
‘S-modules’
or ‘symmetric sequences/families’ in the literature.

The continuous and discrete settings are often related by letting combinatorial objects index data on spaces. That is, one has a combinatorial species
p
, a family of spaces
(\mathcal{X}^{I})_{I\in\textsf{finSet}}
, some chosen data ‘
D
’, and a morphism of species

\eta:\text{p}\to\text{D}(\mathcal{X}^{(-)}).

In addition, species appearing in these two contexts often have compatible algebraic structure, i.e.
\eta
is a homomorphism of the appropriate kind. As a famous example, certain multigraphs index cells of the moduli space of genus zero stable marked curves with nodal singularities, and the indexing is a homomorphism of cyclic operads. More precisely, fundamentally the
space

\mathcal{X}^{(-)}
has algebraic structure, manifesting e.g. as factorization of its boundaries, which endows
\text{D}(\mathcal{X}^{(-)})
with algebraic structure by pushforward/pullback of data.

The main goal of this paper is to rigorously exhibit several of these indexing constructions, but also to provide a theoretical framework for formalizing and systematizing further such examples. We focus on the case of the toric variety of the permutohedron, called
permutohedral space
following
[
Kap93
]
, which may be realized as a moduli space parametrizing strings of stable marked Riemann spheres, glued at the poles
[
LM00
]
,
[
BB11
]
. Our main result is the precise formulation of the indexing of nef line bundles on permutohedral space by generalized permutohedra, or more generally line bundles by Boolean functions, see
Section 8
. In particular, in
8.6
we realize it as a homomorphism of bimonoids. It turns out we actually have to use invertible sheaves instead of line bundles in order to do this faithfully.

This paper is not comprehensive in giving examples of combinatorial objects which index data on permutohedral space. One of the most interesting examples we do not study is the indexing of degree one cycles of (tropical) permutohedral space by loopless valuated matroids, see e.g.
[
Eur20
, Section 2.3]
. This could be studied analogously to our treatment of generalized permutohedra and Boolean functions.

Along the way, we shall require three key elaborations of Joyal species. Firstly, we need a generalization of Joyal species called braid arrangement species, which are required in settings where one does not have ‘factorization’. The reason we shall not enjoy this factorization property is because data of a product of spaces is not necessarily the product of data,

\text{D}(\mathcal{X}\times\mathcal{Y})\neq\text{D}(\mathcal{X})\times\text{D}(\mathcal{Y}).

Braid arrangement species (= Coxeter species of type
A
) are due to Aguiar-Mahajan
[
AM22
, Section 2.3]
, who define species and bimonoids over real reflection hyperplane arrangements, arbitrary real hyperplane arrangements
[
AM20
]
, or even
left-regular bands
. A
composition

F
of
I
is a tuple of nonempty sets
F=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})
such that
I=S_{1}\sqcup\dots\sqcup S_{k}
. A
C
-valued
braid arrangement species
, or just
species
,
p
consists of an object

\textsf{p}[F]\in\textsf{C}

for each composition
F
, equipped with actions of bijections and permutations, see
Section 1.3
. The name ‘braid arrangement species’ is explained by the fact compositions are in natural one-to-one correspondence with faces of the braid hyperplane arrangement. If
C
is a monoidal category, associated to a Joyal species
p
is the
Joyal-theoretic
braid arrangement species
p
, which has

\textsf{p}[F]:=\text{p}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes\text{p}[S_{k}].

See
Section 1.5
. In this way, species generalize Joyal species by allowing the components
\textsf{p}[F]
to be more complicated objects than just these tensor products. In this paper, we find crucial to connecting the setting without factorization with the more classical setting where we do have factorization is the notion of
weakly Joyal-theoretic species
, see
Section 1.5
. An example of a weakly Joyal-theoretic species involves taking external tensor products of sheaves of modules, see
7.2
.

A
bimonoid
in species (when
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Vec}
we tend to say ‘bialgebra’ instead of bimonoid) is a species
h
equipped with a pair of maps

\mu_{F,G}:\textsf{h}[F]\to\textsf{h}[G]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{F,G}:\textsf{h}[G]\to\textsf{h}[F]

for each pair of compositions
F,G
such that
F
contains
G
as braid arrangement faces, called the multiplication and comultiplication respectively. The key axiom for bimonoids, called the
bimonoid axiom
, depends crucially on Tits’ projection product
[
Tit74
]
of braid arrangement faces,
1
1

1

the product for just the braid arrangement appeared independently in
[
EGS75
, Section 4.1]
, where it is used to express causal factorization of time-ordered products
see
Section 2.1
.

A bimonoid
h
is called
Joyal-theoretic
if
h
is a Joyal-theoretic species and its (co)multiplication similarly factorizes, see
Section 2.3
. In this case, setting
F=(S,T)
and
G=(I)
recovers more familiar looking (co)multiplication,

\mu_{S,T}=\mu_{(S,T),(I)}:\textsf{h}[S]\otimes\textsf{h}[T]\to\textsf{h}[I]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{S,T}=\Delta_{(S,T),(I)}:\textsf{h}[I]\to\textsf{h}[S]\otimes\textsf{h}[T].

Bimonoids in species may be formalized as bialgebras over a certain bimonad acting on species. See
[
AM22
, Section 2.10]
for the case
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Vec}
, and see
[
AM20
, Section 3.7]
for the case
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Set}
but in a less structured setting where there is no action of bijections. Aguiar-Mahajan have shown how bimonoids in species provide a deeper and more unified perspective on combinatorial graded Hopf algebras
[
AM10
, Chapter 15]
, and also how these graded Hopf algebra generalize to non-type
A
settings
[
AM22
, Part II]
.

Secondly, we require a vertical categorification of species and bimonoids to allow for
C
to be a
2
-category. This is because the data ‘
D
’ often forms a category

\text{D}(\mathcal{X}^{I})\in\textsf{Cat}

where
Cat
is the
2
-category of
1
-categories. This means we need to develop a theory of species and bimonoids where axioms hold only up to coherent isomorphism. This takes up most of the first part of the paper, and culminates in our definition of a ‘
2
-bimonoid’ in
Section 2.1
. We do not prove any coherence theorems, and in this paper it is only a conjecture that we have all the coherence conditions we should want in the correction definition of a
2
-bimonoid. We leave the conjectural formalization of
2
-bimonoids as bialgebras over a
2
-bimonad to future work.

Thirdly, towards applications to Feynman amplitudes mentioned in the abstract (see e.g.
[
Bro17
]
,
[
Sch18
]
), after completing the indexing of nef invertible sheaves by generalized permutohedra, we would like to construct some kind of ‘bialgebra of global sections of invertible sheaves’. For example, this should be the bialgebra which appears in the factorization identities for Symanzik polynomials. Also, since permutohedral space is a bimonoid, surely the structure sheaf is somehow a bialgebra (recall global regular functions on permutohedral space are all constant). To realize these, we require the notion of
p
-graded set/vector species, see
Section 3.1
. Here,
p
is a species valued in categories, and one has a set/vector space for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
. Similarly, when
h
is a bimonoid valued in categories, one can define
h
-graded bimonoids/bialgebras, see
Section 3.2
.

We now give a brief summary of the paper.
Part I
, which consists of
Section 1
,
Section 2
and
Section 3
, deals with developing our three elaborations on Joyal species described above. The reader might wish to skip ahead to
Part II
, and refer back to the theory building of
Part I
as required.

We begin the application to permutohedral space in
Section 4
by considering
preposets
(=reflexive and transitive relations). The bialgebra of preposets
O
was defined in
[
AM10
, Section 13.1.6]
. See also
[
AA17
, Section 15.4]
. Preposets
p
are significant because they bijectively index cones
\sigma_{p}
of the braid arrangement. By cone duality, they also bijectively index cones
\sigma^{\circ}_{p}=-\sigma^{\vee}_{p}
of the adjoint braid arrangement which are generated by coroots, see
Section 4.7
. Multiplication of preposets corresponds to taking products of coroot cones, and comultiplication of preposets corresponds to taking faces of coroot cones and factorizing, see
4.4
and
4.6
. (See
4.5
for an interpretation in terms of cones of the braid arrangement.)

However, the bialgebra
O
does not have a
Set
-valued bimonoid analog, because the comultiplication of preposets is sometimes ‘inadmissible’ and equals zero. This is a problem for us, especially given the kind of role we want combinatorial objects like preposets to play, mentioned above. The fix is to let the bimonoid of preposets
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
be valued in
pointed
sets instead, or even pointed categories
2
2

2

for us, a
pointed category

\textsf{D}\in\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
is a functor
1_{\textsf{Cat}}\to\textsf{D}
where
1_{\textsf{Cat}}
is the trivial category

\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[I]\in\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
, where morphisms between preposets correspond to inclusion of coroots cones, see
4.1
. Note the distinguished object
\bullet\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[I]
playing the role of zero is not a preposet, rather it is a formal element we add by hand. We make the convention that its corresponding cone is the empty cone
\sigma^{\circ}_{\bullet}:=\emptyset
.

In
Section 5.1
, we proceed to describe the bimonoid structure of permutohedral space ‘algebra first’ as opposed to ‘geometry first’, in order to fully expose the combinatorial structure. (A geometry first description is straightforward using Losev-Manin’s realization of permutohedral space
[
LM00
]
as a certain moduli space parametrizing stable marked strings of Riemann spheres glued at the poles; the multiplication is concatenation of strings of Riemann spheres and the comultiplication is forgetting marked points followed by stabilization.) We construct the
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
-graded
bialgebra
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
, where the vector space associated to
p
is the
\mathbb{C}
-algebra
\mathbb{C}[\text{M}_{p}]
which has basis the integer points of
\sigma^{\circ}_{p}
. The algebraic structure of
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
largely follows from the geometric interpretation of
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
in terms of coroot cones. The multiplication of
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
is given by taking products of integer points, and the comultiplication is given by projecting onto faces and factorizing, see
Section 5.1
.

The toric variety
\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{I}
of the braid arrangement fan over a finite set
I
is constructed by taking charts the affine varieties

U_{F}:=\operatorname{Hom}_{\textsf{Aff}_{\mathbb{C}}}\!\big(\,\ast\,,\,\text{Spec}(\mathbb{C}[\text{M}_{F}])\big)=\operatorname{Hom}_{\mathbb{C}\textsf{Alg}}\!\big(\mathbb{C}[\text{M}_{F}],\mathbb{C}\big)

where
F
is a total preposet on
I
(which are equivalent to compositions of
I
), and gluing along the inclusion of coroot cones. In
Section 5.2
and
Section 5.3
, we get a multiplication/comultiplication on permutohedral space
\mathbb{C}\Sigma
by pulling back points
\mathbb{C}[\text{M}_{F}]\to\mathbb{C}
along the comultiplication/multiplication of
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
. In particular,
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
was a commutative bialgebra, whereas
\mathbb{C}\Sigma
is a cocommutative bimonoid.

From a geometry first perspective, we may reinterpret
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
as follows. For each preposet
p
, the toric variety
U_{p}
of the restriction of the braid fan to
\sigma_{p}
is naturally an open set
U_{p}\subseteq\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{I}
. We have a natural identification

\mathbb{C}[\text{M}_{p}]=\big\{\text{regular functions $U_{p}\to\mathbb{C}$}\big\}.

Then the multiplication/comultiplication of
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
is given by pulling back regular functions along the comultiplication/multiplication of permutohedral space
\mathbb{C}\Sigma
.

In
Section 6
, we formalize and generalize this structure as follows. We have the bimonoid of opens
\textsf{Op}^{\text{op}}
, given by

\textsf{Op}^{\text{op}}[F]:=\big\{\text{Zariski open sets of }\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{S_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{S_{k}}\big\}

where the multiplication/comultiplication is given by pulling back opens along the comultiplication/multiplication of permutohedral space. In
6.3
, we show the indexing of opens by preposets may be formalized as a homomorphism of bimonoids

\textsf{O}_{\bullet}\to\textsf{Op}^{\text{op}},\qquad p\mapsto U_{p}.

We have the
\textsf{Op}^{\text{op}}
-graded bialgebra
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{Op}}^{\text{op}}
, where the vector space associated to an open
U
is the space of regular functions on
U
, and the multiplication/comultiplication is given by pulling back regular functions along the comultiplication/multiplication of permutohedral space. We may then pullback the grading of
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{Op}}^{\text{op}}
along
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}\to\textsf{Op}^{\text{op}}
, which recovers the
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
-graded bialgebra
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
. In
3.1
, we show that graded bimonoids/bialgebras can always be pulled-back along homomorphisms in this way.

We have that
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{Op}}^{\text{op}}
is not Joyal-theoretic, whereas
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
is. Thus,
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
may be viewed as a kind of ‘algebraic Joyalization’ of
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{Op}}^{\text{op}}
by restricting its structure to that which is seen by preposets. Of course,
\mathbb{C}\textsf{{O}}
may be viewed in isolation as Joyal-theoretic, which is what we did in
Section 5.1
. However, the origin (or at least interpretation) of its algebraic structure, of pulling back opens and regular functions, is inherently non Joyal-theoretic.
3
3

3

An analogous construction can be found in
[
LNO19
]
, where algebraic Joyalization happens by taking a quotient, and once again the algebraic structure (this time a Lie algebra) has its origin in a non Joyal-theoretic setting. The quotient is by the so-called Steinmann relations of axiomatic QFT.

In
Section 7
and
Section 8
, we consider an analog of the above but in the setting of the sheaf topos
Sh
of
\textsf{Op}^{\text{op}}
. We consider sheaves of modules instead of opens, global sections instead of regular functions, and Boolean functions
BF
(or generalized permutohedra
GP
) instead of preposets. This goes as follows.

Let
\mathcal{O}_{F}
denote the structure sheaf of
\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{S_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{S_{k}}
. We have the bimonoid of modules
Mod
, given by

\textsf{Mod}[F]:=\big\{\text{sheaves of $\mathcal{O}_{F}$-modules on }\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{S_{1}}\times\dots\times\mathbb{C}\Sigma^{S_{k}}\big\}

where the multiplication/comultiplication is given by pulling back modules along the comultiplication/multiplication of permutohedral space. This is a ‘
2
-bimonoid’, with algebraic structure holding only up to coherent isomorphism. In
Section 8.5
and
Section 8.6
, we construct a (lax, but strong) homomorphism of bimonoids

\textsf{BF}\to\textsf{Mod},\qquad z\mapsto\mathcal{O}_{z}

with essential image the invertible sheaves of permutohedral space, where
BF
is the bimonoid of so-called Boolean functions, see
[
AA17
, Section 12.1]
. We prove this is a homomorphism in
8.6
. The restriction to generalized permutohedra

\textsf{GP}\hookrightarrow\textsf{BF}

indexes the nef invertible sheaves. If we want this homomorphism to be injective, so that
BF
and
GP
are faithfully interpreted, we have to use invertible sheaves here and
not
line bundles. In addition, the invertible sheaves we construct
\mathcal{O}_{z}
have to be ‘closed’ under pullback along the (co)multiplication of permutohedral space. In particular, the usual construction of invertible sheaves on toric varieties as subsheaves of the sheaf of rational functions is not sufficient.

We have the
Mod
-graded bialgebra
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{Mod}}
, where the vector space associated to a module
M
is the space of global sections
s\in M
, and the multiplication/comultiplication of
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{Mod}}
is given by pulling back global sections along the comultiplication/multiplication of permutohedral space, see
Section 7.4
. We may then pullback the grading of
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{Mod}}
along
\textsf{BF}\to\textsf{Mod}
, to give Joyal-theoretic bialgebras
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{BF}}
and
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{GP}}
, see
Section 8.7
. In particular,
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{BF}}
is the bialgebra of global sections of invertible sheaves. As with
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{O}}
,
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{BF}}
may be considered in isolation as Joyal-theoretic, however its algebraic structure has come from an inherently non Joyal-theoretic setting.

The restriction
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{GP}}
of
\mathbb{C}\textbf{{BF}}
to generalized permutohedra has a nice description. The space of global sections of the invertible sheaf
\mathcal{O}_{\mathfrak{p}}
of a generalized permutohedron
\mathfrak{p}
is naturality the vector space with basis the integer points of
\mathfrak{p}
. Then pullback of global sections along the multiplication of permutohedral space corresponds to taking (integer points in) faces of generalized permutohedra and factorizing, and the pullback of global sections along the comultiplication of permutohedral space corresponds to taking products of (integer points in) generalized permutohedra. See
[
AA17
, Section 5.1]
for a description of these operations at the level of the indexing generalized permutohedra.

Future Work

The main motivation for this work concerns Euclidean Feynman amplitudes in the Schwinger parametrization, which become integrals over permutohedral space if one ‘blows-up everything’ in the resolution of singularities
[
Bro17
]
,
[
Sch18
]
. Then the Hopf algebra structure of Feynman graphs, first appearing in the work of Connes-Kreimer, coincides with that induced by the bimonoid structure of permutohedral space. After conversations with Franz Herzog, the author understands that a conjectural Hopf algebra structure of Feynman amplitudes in
Minkowski signature
may require non Joyal-theoretic bimonoids throughout. For example, can the forest-like treatment of IR divergences in
[
Ma20
]
be understood in terms of non Joyal-theoretic bimonoids?

In previous work of the author, the construction of the perturbative S-matrix scheme in causal perturbation theory was formalized in terms of bimonoids in species
[
Nor20
]
. Study the role permutohedral space may play there, via its interpretations as the space of configurations on a worldline (tropical) or worldsheet (complex).

There are other combinatorial bimonoids which can be geometrically interpreted over permutohedral space. For example, the indexing of degree one cycles of tropical permutohedral space by valuated loopless matroids. These cycles can be pulled back along the (co)multiplication of permutohedral space, which is compatible with the bimonoid structure on loopless matroids as given here
[
AM10
, Section 13.8.5]
. This suggests the question; can the Grassmannian or Dressian be equipped with bimonoid structure?

The author’s understanding is that cyclic operads are
not
operads (if by ‘operad’ we mean a monoid with respect to a plethystic tensor product, and by plethystic tensor product we mean the categorified Fourier transform of a composition of analytic endofunctors). Rather they are
algebras
over a certain monad acting on Joyal species, and are thus analogous to the (bi)monoids of this paper. Using Aguiar-Mahajan’s theory of bimonoids with respect to left-regular bands
[
AM20
, Section 3.9]
, can the moduli space of genus zero stable marked curves with nodal singularities be realized as a bimonoid?

The type
A
full flag variety has analogous bimonoid structure, and this may be interesting to study. Copies of permutohedral space sit inside the full flag variety as
sub-bimonoids
.

Acknowledgments.

We thank Adrian Ocneanu for his support and useful discussions, and Nick Early for useful discussions. We thank Marcelo Aguiar and Swapneel Mahajan for the sharing of unpublished notes on the topic of
p
-graded species. We thank Penn State maths department for their support.

Part I
Species and Bimonoids Background

1.
Species

1.1.
Compositions of Finite Sets

Let
I
be a finite set. For
k\in\mathbb{N}
, let

(k):=\{1,\dots,k\}

equipped with the ordering
1>\dots>k
. A
composition

F
of
I
of
length

l(F)=k
is a surjective function
F:I\to(k)
. The set of all compositions of
I
is denoted
\Sigma[I]
,

\Sigma[I]:=\bigsqcup_{k\in\mathbb{N}}\big\{\text{surjective functions}\ F:I\to(k)\big\}.

We often denote compositions by
k
-tuples

F=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})

where
S_{i}:=F^{-1}(i)
,
1\leq i\leq k
. The
S_{i}
are called the
lumps
of
F
. In particular, we have the length one composition
(I)
for
I\neq\emptyset
, and the length zero composition
(\,)
which is the unique composition of the empty set. Note the
1
-tuple consisting of the empty set
(\emptyset)
does not correspond to a composition. For compositions
F,G\in\Sigma[I]
, we write
G\leq F
if
G
can be obtained from
F
by iteratively merging contiguous lumps.

Given a decomposition
I\!=S\sqcup T
of
I
(
S
,
T
can be empty), for
H=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})
a composition of
S
and
K=(T_{1},\dots,T_{l})
a composition of
T
, their
concatenation

H;K
is the composition of
I
given by

H;K:=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k},T_{1},\dots,T_{l}).

For
S\subseteq I
and
H=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})\in\Sigma[I]
, the
restriction

H|_{S}
of
H
to
S
is the composition of
S
given by

H|_{S}:=(S_{1}\cap S,\dots,S_{k}\cap S)_{+}

where
(-)_{+}
means we delete any sets from the list which are the empty set.

1.2.
Joyal Species

Before describing braid arrangement species, we first recall the more classical notion of Joyal species, going back to
[
Joy81
]
,
[
Joy86
]
. The connection between Joyal species and braid arrangement species is described in
Section 1.5
.

Let
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
denote the monoidal category with objects finite sets
I
, morphisms bijective functions
\sigma
, and monoidal product disjoint union. For
C
a category, a
C
-valued

Joyal (co)species

p
is a
C
-valued
(co)presheaf on
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
,

\text{p}:(\textsf{finSet}^{\times})^{\text{(op)}}\to\textsf{C},\qquad I\mapsto\text{p}[I],\quad\sigma\mapsto\text{p}[\sigma].

In particular, for
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Set},\textsf{Vec}\}
, we say
Joyal set (co)species
and
Joyal vector (co)species
.

This definition allows for
C
to be a higher category. For example, in this paper we shall often have
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Cat}
, where
Cat
is the
2
-category of
1
-categories. In general, when
C
is a
2
-category
, a
C
-valued Joyal (co)species should be a
C
-valued
2
-(co)presheaf
4
4

4

i.e. pseudofunctor, a.k.a. strong
2
-functor, see e.g.
[
Joh02
, Definition B1.2.1]
on
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
. Such objects might be called
‘
2
-(co)species’
, and are also known as ‘stuff types’ when
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Grpd}
. We will just say ‘
C
-valued (co)species’, where the fact it is a
2
-(co)species is understood from the fact
C
is a
2
-category. For simplicity, in this paper we add the condition that
C
-valued Joyal (co)species are strictly unital, i.e. they preserve the identities of
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
strictly.

If
C
has the additional structure of a monoidal category
\textsf{C}=(\textsf{C},\otimes,1_{\textsf{C}})
, a
C
-valued Joyal (co)species
p
is called
connected
if

\text{p}[\emptyset]=1_{\textsf{C}}.

In this paper, we only consider connected Joyal (co)species.
5
5

5

the generic
not-necessarily-connected
setting would require a more general theory which uses decompositions in place of compositions, where a
decomposition

F
of
I
is a function
F:I\to(k)
(not necessarily surjective), although the existence of this more general theory is currently just a conjecture for the author

In
[
AM10
, Chapter 15]
, Aguiar-Mahajan showed that a deeper, more explanatory, and more unified perspective on combinatorial (
\mathbb{N}
-graded) Hopf algebras is that they are decategorifications (namely
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}\to\mathbb{N}
) of Hopf monoids constructed internal to vector species, using the Day convolution as the tensor product. Moreover, many of these Hopf monoids are linearizations of certain
set-theoretic
bimonoids constructed in connected set species. (The set-theoretic bimonoids are not internal bimonoids, rather they are correctly formalized as bialgebras over a certain bimonad.)

Example 1.1
.

The mapping
I\mapsto\Sigma[I]
extends to a connected Joyal set species
\Sigma
(where
Set
is a cartesian category) given by

\Sigma:(\textsf{finSet}^{\times})^{\text{op}}\to\textsf{Set},\qquad I\mapsto\Sigma[I],\quad\sigma\mapsto\Sigma[\sigma]

where
\Sigma[\sigma](F):=F\circ\sigma
. Then
\Sigma
becomes a set-theoretic bimonoid with multiplication concatenation of compositions and comultiplication restriction of compositions. Its covariant linearization has decategorified image in graded vector spaces the graded Hopf algebra of noncommutative symmetric functions
NSym
, which has been widely studied. As we shall see, the bimonoid structure of
\Sigma
is a precursor to the bimonoid structure of permutohedral space, which is obtained by replacing
(k)
with a string of
k
-many Riemann spheres, glued at the poles.

1.3.
Braid Arrangement Species

We now describe a generalization of connected Joyal species called braid arrangement species. As we shall see, braid arrangement species are required in settings where one does not have certain ‘factorization’. Factorization is implicit in Joyal species. This generalization is due to
Aguiar-Mahajan

[
AM20
]
,
[
AM22
]
, who focus on the cases
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Set},\textsf{Vec}\}
. In this paper, we require a vertical categorification of Aguiar-Mahajan’s work to allow for
C
to be a
2
-category.

As with Joyal species, one can define braid arrangement species so they take values in any higher category
C
. In this paper,
C
will either be the
2
-category
of (strict)
1
-categories
Cat
6
6

6

we let
Cat
be sufficiently large, i.e. ‘very large’, to contain e.g.
Set
or the
2
-category
of pointed categories
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
7
7

7

For us, a
pointed category

D
is a functor of the form
1_{\textsf{Cat}}\to\textsf{D}
, where
D
is a (strict)
1
-category and
1_{\textsf{Cat}}
is the trivial category. The image of
1_{\textsf{Cat}}
is called the
distinguished object
of
D
, which we often denote by
\bullet
. A functor between pointed categories is required to preserve the distinguished object.
, and occasionally the category of sets
Set
or the category of vector spaces
Vec
over a field
\Bbbk
.
8
8

8

p
-
graded species
, defined in
Section 3.1
, are valued exclusively in
Set
and
Vec
, and really, our braid arrangement species valued in
Set
and
Vec
come from quotienting out the grading of a
p
-graded species, see e.g.
5.1
However
Cat
and
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
are moreover
strict

2
-categories, meaning unitors and associators are trivial. Therefore we define braid arrangement species in the setting of strict
2
-categories only, since we do not need to go any higher.

When a bijection
\sigma:J\to I
between finite sets is understood and there is no ambiguity, given a composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
, we let

F^{\prime}:=F\circ\sigma\,\in\Sigma[J].

Let
\text{Sym}_{k}
denote the symmetric group on
(k)=\{1,\dots,k\}
. When a permutation
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{k}
is understood and there is no ambiguity, given a composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
of length
k
, we let

\widetilde{F}:=\beta\circ F\,\in\Sigma[I].

When composable bijections
K\xrightarrow{\tau}J\xrightarrow{\sigma}I
are understood, we let
F^{\prime\prime}:=F\circ(\sigma\circ\tau)
. Similarly, when permutations
\beta,\gamma\in\text{Sym}_{k}
are understood, we let
\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}:=(\gamma\circ\beta)\circ F
. We have the following five basic facts, which tell us that compositions form a bimodule under the action of bijections and permutations. The action of bijections and permutations commute

\widetilde{(F^{\prime})}=(\widetilde{F})^{\prime}

(from now on let
\widetilde{F}^{\prime}:=\widetilde{(F^{\prime})}=(\widetilde{F})^{\prime}
). The action of bijections is a right action,

(F^{\prime})^{\prime}=F^{\prime\prime}\quad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\quad F^{\prime}=F\quad\text{if}\quad\sigma=\text{id}_{I}.

The action of permutations is a left action,

\widetilde{(\widetilde{F})}=\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}\quad\qquad\text{and}\qquad\quad\widetilde{F}=F\quad\text{if}\quad\beta=\text{id}_{(k)}.

For
C
a strict
2
-category, a
C
-valued
(strong, strictly unital)
braid arrangement species
, or just
species
,
p
consists of an object
\textsf{p}[F]\in\textsf{C}
for each composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
,
I\in\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
, a morphism (or
1
-cell)

\textsf{p}[\sigma,F]:\textsf{p}[F]\to\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]

for each bijection of finite sets
\sigma:J\to I\,\in\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
and composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
, and a morphism

\textsf{p}[F,\beta]:\textsf{p}[F]\to\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]

for each permutation
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and composition
F
of length
k
. When there is no ambiguity, we often abbreviate

\textsf{p}[I]:=\textsf{p}[(I)],\qquad\textsf{p}[\sigma]:=\textsf{p}[\sigma,(I)],\qquad\textsf{p}[\beta]:=\textsf{p}[(I),\beta]

for
I\neq\emptyset
, and even just

\sigma:=\textsf{p}[\sigma,F],\qquad\beta:=\textsf{p}[F,\beta].

In addition, we require an invertible
2
-cell called
(\sigma,\beta)
-
functoriality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{p}[\sigma,F,\beta]}

an invertible
2
-cell (contravariant)
\sigma
-
functoriality
and a trivial
2
-cell
strictly
\sigma
-unitary

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\textsf{p}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{I}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{p}[\sigma,\tau,F]}

and an invertible
2
-cell (covariant)
\beta
-
functoriality
and a trivial
2
-cell
strictly
\beta
-unitary

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\textsf{p}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{(k)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{p}[F,\beta,\gamma]}

When there is no ambiguity, we often abbreviate

(\sigma,\beta):=\textsf{p}[\sigma,F,\beta],\qquad(\sigma,\tau):=\textsf{p}[\sigma,\tau,F],\qquad(\beta,\gamma):=\textsf{p}[F,\beta,\gamma].

We require such
2
-cells for all meaningful choices of
F,\sigma,\tau,\beta,\gamma
, with the requirement that
\sigma,\tau,\beta,\gamma\neq\text{id}
.
9
9

9

alternatively, one can include these cases in the definition, but then needs to add coherence conditions saying that all the resulting
2
-cells are trivial, e.g.
\textsf{p}[\sigma,F,\text{id}_{(k)}]=\text{id}_{\textsf{p}[\sigma,F]}
For example, we have a
\sigma
-functoriality
2
-cell
(\sigma,\tau)
for each pair of composable non-identity bijections
K\xrightarrow{\tau}J\xrightarrow{\sigma}I
and choice of composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
. If all the
2
-cells are trivial, i.e. the above five diagrams commute on the nose, then the species is called
strict
. If
C
is a
1
-category, for example
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Set},\textsf{Vec}\}
, then a
C
-valued species must be strict.

We impose four coherence conditions on the
2
-cells
(\sigma,\beta)
,
(\sigma,\tau)
,
(\beta,\gamma)
. We require trivial
3
-cells
(\sigma,\tau,\beta)
-coherence
and
(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)
-coherence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tau,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma\circ\tau,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\gamma\circ\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\circ\beta}

and trivial
3
-cells
(\sigma,\tau,\upsilon)
-coherence
and
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)
-coherence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{p}[F]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime\prime\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!(\sigma\circ\tau,\upsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\upsilon}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\tau\circ\upsilon)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \,(\sigma,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\tau,\upsilon)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{p}[F]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}}].}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!(\gamma\circ\beta,\delta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta,\delta\circ\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \,(\beta,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma,\delta)}

for all meaningful choices. For example, we have a
(\sigma,\tau,\beta)
-coherence trivial
3
-cell for each pair of composable non-identity bijections
K\xrightarrow{\tau}J\xrightarrow{\sigma}I
, choice of composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
, and choice of non-identity permutation
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{k}
where
k=l(F)
.

Let us make these coherence conditions fully explicit in the case
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, at the level of components of the
2
-cells (although in this paper, all
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
-valued species will in fact be strict). For this, the following notation will be useful. When an appropriate bijection
\sigma
is understood and there is no ambiguity, given a (generalized) element
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
in some component of a species
p
, we abbreviate

\mathtt{a}^{\prime}:=\sigma(\mathtt{a})\,\in\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}].

When composable bijections
\sigma\circ\tau
are understood, we abbreviate
\mathtt{a}^{\prime\prime}:=\sigma\circ\tau(\mathtt{a})\in\textsf{p}[F^{\prime\prime}]
. Similarly, when an appropriate permutation
\beta
is understood and there is no ambiguity, we abbreviate

\widetilde{\mathtt{a}}:=\beta(\mathtt{a})\,\in\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}].

When permutations
\beta,\gamma\in\text{Sym}_{k}
are understood, we abbreviate
\tilde{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}:=\gamma\circ\beta(\mathtt{a})\in\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]
.

Then, in the case
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, the
2
-cell
(\sigma,\beta)
consists of a natural isomorphism in the category
\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]

{(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}}:\widetilde{(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}\to(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})^{\prime}

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
,
(\sigma,\tau)
consists of a natural isomorphism in
\textsf{p}[F^{\prime\prime}]

{(\sigma,\tau)}_{\mathtt{a}}:(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})^{\prime}\to\mathtt{a}^{\prime\prime}

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
, and
(\beta,\gamma)
consists of a natural isomorphism in
\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]

{(\beta,\gamma)}_{\mathtt{a}}:\widetilde{(\widetilde{\mathtt{a}})}\to\widetilde{\widetilde{\mathtt{a}}}

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
. Recall being natural isomorphisms means that e.g. in the case of
{(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}}
, we have

{(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{b}}\circ\widetilde{(\mathtt{f}^{\prime})}=(\tilde{\mathtt{f}})^{\prime}\circ{(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}}

for all morphisms
\mathtt{f}:\mathtt{a}\to\mathtt{b}\in\textsf{p}[F]
. Then
(\sigma,\tau,\beta)
-coherence says

{(\sigma\circ\tau,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\widetilde{(\sigma,\tau)}}_{\mathtt{a}}={(\sigma,\tau)}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ(\sigma,\beta)^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{(\tau,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}

(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)
-coherence says

(\sigma,\gamma\circ\beta)_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\beta,\gamma)_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}=(\beta,\gamma)^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\gamma)_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\widetilde{(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}}

(\sigma,\tau,\upsilon)
-coherence says

(\sigma\circ\tau,\upsilon)_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}=(\sigma,\tau\circ\upsilon)_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\tau,\upsilon)_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}

and
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)
-coherence says

(\gamma\circ\beta,\delta)_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\widetilde{(\beta,\gamma)}_{\mathtt{a}}=(\beta,\delta\circ\gamma)_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\gamma,\delta)_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}

for all meaningful choices. Note here, each instance of a prime or tilde is referring to a bijection or permutation unambiguously. For example,
(\sigma,\beta)_{\mathtt{a}}
is a morphism in the category
\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]
and so the prime
(\sigma,\beta)^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}
appearing in
(\sigma,\tau,\beta)
-coherence must be referring to
\tau
and not
\sigma
.

Remark 1.1
.

The name ‘braid arrangement species’ comes from the fact that compositions
F
are in natural one-to-one correspondence with the chambers of the braid hyperplane arrangement. Therefore we might say that
p
consists of an object
\textsf{p}[F]
for each chamber of the braid arrangement. More generally, we have the remarkable fact that species (equipped with actions of the
\beta
’s but not
\sigma
’s), and bimonoids in species, make sense with respect to any real hyperplane arrangement, or even any left-regular band
[
AM20
]
. There is also a more structured theory involving reflection hyperplane arrangements
[
AM22
]
.

Remark 1.2
.

We may formalize
C
-valued braid arrangement species as certain strictly unitary pseudofunctors, a.k.a. strictly unitary strong
2
-functors, on the action groupoid for the action of bijections
\sigma
and permutations
\beta
on compositions. See
[
AM22
, Section 2.1.1]
for an explicit definition of this groupoid. This is similar to Joyal species, where the action groupoid
\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
was for the action of just bijections on finite sets.

Example 1.2
.

The primordial
Set
-valued species is the
exponential species

E
, given by

\textsf{E}[F]:=\{F\},\qquad\textsf{E}[\sigma,F](F):=F^{\prime},\qquad\textsf{E}[F,\beta](F):=\widetilde{F}.

Up to isomorphism, the exponential species is the unique species
p
such that each component
\textsf{p}[F]
is a singleton set. The name ‘exponential species’ comes from the connection between species and formal power series.

Example 1.3
.

We have the
Set
-valued species of compositions
\mathsf{\Sigma}
, given by

\mathsf{\Sigma}\big[(\,)\big]:=1_{\textsf{Set}},\qquad\mathsf{\Sigma}[F]:=\big\{(H_{1},\dots,H_{k})\ \big|\ H_{i}\in\Sigma[S_{i}]\big\}

where
F=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})
, and

\mathsf{\Sigma}[\sigma,F](H_{1},\dots,H_{k}):=(H^{\prime}_{1},\dots,H^{\prime}_{k}),\qquad\mathsf{\Sigma}[F,\beta](H_{1},\dots,H_{k}):=(H_{\beta^{-1}(1)},\dots,H_{\beta^{-1}(k)}).

Here,
H^{\prime}_{i}=H_{i}\circ\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}}
where
S^{\prime}_{i}=\sigma^{-1}(S_{i})
. We also have a
Cat
-valued version of
\mathsf{\Sigma}
by including a single morphism

(K_{1},\dots,K_{k})\to(H_{1},\dots,H_{k})

whenever
K_{i}\leq H_{i}
for all
1\leq i\leq k
. See
1.5
for the connection to the Joyal species of compositions
\Sigma
.

Dually, we may define
C
-valued
cospecies

p
, which consist of objects
\textsf{p}[F]
, morphisms
\textsf{p}[\sigma,F]
,
\textsf{p}[F,\beta]
, and
2
-cells
\textsf{p}[\sigma,F,\beta]
,
\textsf{p}[\sigma,\tau,F]
,
\textsf{p}[F,\beta,\gamma]
as with species, however the source and target of
\textsf{p}[\sigma,F]
and
\textsf{p}[F,\beta]
are switched. Thus, cospecies are covariant in the bijections
\sigma
and contravariant in the permutations
\beta
. The coherence conditions we impose are the analogous four trivial
3
-cells.

We make this explicit. In the setting of cospecies, we adopt the following alternative notation. For
\sigma:J\to I
,
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{k}
, and
F\in\Sigma[J]
where
l(F)=k
, we instead let

F^{\prime}:=F\circ\sigma^{-1}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\widetilde{F}:=\beta^{-1}\circ F.

For
C
a strict
2
-category, a
C
-valued
cospecies
consists of an object
\textsf{p}[F]\in\textsf{C}
for each composition
F
, a morphism

\textsf{p}[\sigma,F^{\prime}]:\textsf{p}[F]\to\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]

for each bijection of finite sets
\sigma:J\to I
and composition
F\in\Sigma[J]
, and a morphism

\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F},\beta]:\textsf{p}[F]\to\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]

for each permutation
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{k}
,
k\in\mathbb{N}
, and composition
F
of length
k
. Then
(\sigma,\beta)
-functoriality is an invertible
2
-cell as before, however the
\sigma
-functoriality and
\beta
-functoriality
2
-cells now have the form
\textsf{p}[\sigma,F]\circ\textsf{p}[\tau,F]\Rightarrow\textsf{p}[\sigma\circ\tau,F]
and
\textsf{p}[\beta,F]\circ\textsf{p}[\gamma,F]\Rightarrow\textsf{p}[\gamma\circ\beta,F]
respectively, i.e. the order of composition of the image is switched. After constructing the analogous trivial
3
-cells, for
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
and at the level of components of the
2
-cells, we find that
(\sigma,\tau,\beta)
-coherence now says

{(\sigma\circ\tau,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\widetilde{(\sigma,\tau)}}_{\mathtt{a}}={(\sigma,\tau)}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\underbrace{(\tau,\beta)^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}}_{\text{ $\sigma$ and $\tau$ switched }}

and
(\beta,\gamma,\sigma)
-coherence now says

(\sigma,\gamma\circ\beta)_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\beta,\gamma)_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}=(\beta,\gamma)^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\underbrace{(\sigma,\beta)_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\widetilde{(\sigma,\gamma)}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{\text{ $\beta$ and $\gamma$ switched }}.

Whereas
(\sigma,\tau,\upsilon)
-coherence and
(\beta,\gamma,\delta)
-coherence are unchanged.

As we shall see, cospecies arise by taking contravariant data on a species
p
, with the action of bijections and permutations being given by pulling back data along the corresponding actions of
p
. We have the following basic, discrete example of this; of contravariantly linearizing set species to obtain vector cospecies.

Example 1.4
.

Let
p
be a
Set
-valued species with finite discrete components
\textsf{p}[F]
, probably sets of labeled combinatorial objects of a certain kind. Then we have the
Vec
-valued cospecies
\textbf{{p}}^{\ast}
given by

\textbf{{p}}^{\ast}[F]:=\big\{\text{functions}\ f:\textsf{p}[F]\to\Bbbk\big\}

\textbf{{p}}^{\ast}[\sigma,F](f):=f\circ\textsf{p}[\sigma,F],\qquad\textbf{{p}}^{\ast}[F,\beta](f):=f\circ\textsf{p}[F,\beta].

Note the fact
\textbf{{p}}^{\ast}
is a
Vec
-valued cospecies, i.e. the five functoriality
2
-cells commute, follows immediately from the fact that
p
is a species, and that pullback of functions is functorial in the function one pulls backs along.

We shall emulate this construction, but in a continuous setting, several times in this paper. If the pullback of data is functorial only up to isomorphism, our cospecies will need to have non-trivial
2
-cells.

1.4.
Lax Morphisms of Species

We continue to let
C
be a strict
2
-category. A
lax morphism
, or just
morphism
, of
C
-valued species
\eta:\textsf{p}\to\textsf{x}
consists of a morphism

\eta_{F}:\textsf{p}[F]\to\textsf{x}[F]

for each composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
,
I\in\textsf{finSet}^{\times}
, together with
2
-cells

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[\widetilde{F}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\sigma,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F,\beta}}

for each non-identity bijection
\sigma:J\to I
and non-identity permutation
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{l(F)}
. We often abbreviate

\sigma_{F}:=\eta_{\sigma,F}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{F}:=\eta_{F,\beta}

and in the totally lumped case

\eta_{I}:=\eta_{(I)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\sigma_{I}:=\eta_{\sigma,(I)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\beta_{I}:=\eta_{(I),\beta}.

In addition, we require a trivial
3
-cell
(\sigma,\beta)
-coherence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\beta)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\beta)}

and trivial
3
-cells
\sigma
-coherence
and
\beta
-coherence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F^{\prime\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F^{\prime\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F^{\prime\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\tau)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma\circ\tau)_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ\tau}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{p}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\beta,\gamma)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma_{\widetilde{F}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\gamma\circ\beta)_{F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\circ\beta}

for all meaningful choices. We have a well-defined composition of morphisms by composing
2
-cells
. A morphism is called
strong
, respectively
strict
, if all the
2
-cells
\sigma_{F}
and
\beta_{F}
are isomorphisms, respectively identities. If
C
is a
1
-category, then all morphisms are strict.

In the case
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, let us make morphisms explicit at the level of components of the
2
-cells. We have that
\sigma_{F}
consists of a natural morphism in
\textsf{x}[F^{\prime}]

{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}}:\big(\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})\big)^{\prime}\to\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
, and
\beta_{F}
consists of a natural morphism in
\textsf{x}[\widetilde{F}]

\beta_{\mathtt{a}}:\widetilde{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}\to\eta_{F}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
. Then
(\sigma,\beta)
-coherence says

\eta_{\widetilde{F}^{\prime}}\big((\sigma,\beta)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ\beta_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}}={\sigma}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\beta^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\beta)_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}

\sigma
-coherence says

{{(\sigma\circ\tau)}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}=\eta_{F^{\prime\prime}}\big((\sigma,\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\tau}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ{\sigma}^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}

and
\beta
-coherence says

{{(\gamma\circ\beta)}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\beta,\gamma)_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}=\eta_{\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}}\big((\beta,\gamma)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\gamma}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\widetilde{\beta}_{\mathtt{a}}.

Remark 1.3
.

If we formalize species as functors as in
1.2
, lax morphisms of species are lax natural transformations of functors.

The definition of morphisms for cospecies is analogous, with the same
2
-cells. Then
(\sigma,\beta)
-coherence
is unchanged, however
\sigma
-coherence becomes

(\sigma\circ\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}=\eta_{F^{\prime\prime}}\big((\sigma,\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\underbrace{\sigma_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ\tau^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{\text{$\sigma$ and $\tau$ switched}}

and
\beta
-coherence becomes

{{(\gamma\circ\beta)}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\beta,\gamma)_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}=\eta_{\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}}\big((\beta,\gamma)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\underbrace{{\beta}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\widetilde{\gamma}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{\text{$\beta$ and $\gamma$ switched}}\!\!\!\!\!\!\!.

1.5.
Joyal-Theoretic Species

In this section, we describe the connection between connected Joyal species and braid arrangement species. In particular, we show the sense in which braid arrangement species are a generalization of connected Joyal species.

We now suppose that
C
has the additional structure of a symmetric monoidal
2
-category

\textsf{C}=(\textsf{C},\otimes,1_{\textsf{C}})
. In this paper, recall we shall have

\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Set},\textsf{Vec},\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}.

These are all symmetric monoidal categories as follows. We let
Set
and
Cat
be cartesian categories,
Vec
is equipped with the tensor product of vector spaces, and
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
is equipped with the smash product for the cartesian product of categories, see e.g.
[
EM09
, Construction 4.19]
. Therefore for the purposes of this paper, we may assume that the monoidal product
\otimes:\textsf{C}\times\textsf{C}\to\textsf{C}
is a strict
2
-functor, and that the associators, unitors and braiding are all strict 2-natural transformations.

Given a connected
C
-valued Joyal species
p
, we have an associated
C
-valued species
\textsf{p}=\textsf{Br}(\text{p})
given by

\textsf{p}\big[(\,)\big]:=1_{\textsf{C}},\qquad\textsf{p}\big[\text{id}_{\emptyset},(\,)\big]:=\text{id}_{1_{\textsf{C}}},

\textsf{p}[F]:=\text{p}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes\text{p}[S_{k}],\qquad\textsf{p}[\sigma,F]:=\text{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{1}}]\otimes\dots\otimes\text{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{k}}]

and the action of permutations
\beta
, which are then morphisms of the form

\textsf{p}[F,\beta]:\text{p}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes\text{p}[S_{k}]\,\to\,\text{p}[S_{\beta^{-1}(1)}]\otimes\dots\otimes\text{p}[S_{\beta^{-1}(k)}],

are the isomorphisms given by the braiding of
C
, i.e. permuting tensor factors. If
C
is just a
1
-category, we are done. Otherwise we need functoriality
2
-cells, which are given by

\textsf{p}[\sigma,F,\beta]:=\text{id},\qquad\textsf{p}[\sigma,\tau,F]:=\text{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{1}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{1}}]\otimes\dots\otimes\text{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{k}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{k}}],\qquad\textsf{p}[F,\beta,\gamma]:=\text{id}

where
\text{p}[\sigma,\tau]
is the compositor
2
-cell
\text{p}[\tau]\circ\text{p}[\sigma]\Rightarrow\text{p}[\sigma\circ\tau]
of
p
.

Going in the opposite direction, given a
C
-valued species
p
, we have the connected
C
-valued Joyal species
\text{p}=\text{Joy}(\textsf{p})
obtained by restricting its structure to the totally lumped components
F=(I)
, and thus throwing away the action of the
\beta
’s.

Remark 1.4
.

Both
Br
and
Joy
extend to functors between Joyal species and braid arrangement species, however they will not feature in this paper.

The
Joyalization

\breve{\textsf{p}}:=\textsf{Br}(\text{Joy}(\textsf{p}))
of a species
p
is given by

\breve{\textsf{p}}\big[(\,)\big]:=1_{\textsf{C}},\qquad\breve{\textsf{p}}\big[\text{id}_{\emptyset},(\,)\big]:=\text{id}_{1_{\textsf{C}}},

\breve{\textsf{p}}[F]:=\textsf{p}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes\textsf{p}[S_{k}],\qquad\breve{\textsf{p}}[\sigma,F]:=\textsf{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{1}}]\otimes\dots\otimes\textsf{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{k}}]

\breve{\textsf{p}}[\sigma,F,\beta]:=\text{id},\qquad\breve{\textsf{p}}[\sigma,\tau,F]:=\textsf{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{1}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{1}},(S_{1})]\otimes\dots\otimes\textsf{p}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{k}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{k}},(S_{k})],\qquad\breve{\textsf{p}}[F,\beta,\gamma]:=\text{id}

with the action of the
\beta
given by permuting tensor factors. A
weakly Joyal-theoretic species
is a species
p
equipped with a morphism ‘
prod
’ of the form

\text{prod}:\breve{\textsf{p}}\to\textsf{p}

such that
\text{prod}_{I}=\text{id}_{\textsf{p}[I]}
. As we shall see, weakly
Joyal-theoretic
species provide a bridge between connected Joyal species and braid arrangement species. If
prod
is an isomorphism, we say
p
is
strongly Joyal-theoretic
. If
prod
is an identity map, we say
p
is
strictly Joyal-theoretic
, abbreviated
Joyal-theoretic
in this paper.
10
10

10

this breaks the principle of equivalence, but will nevertheless be useful
(Equivalently, a Joyal-theoretic species is a species which is in the strict image of the functor
Br
, equivalently a species which is equal to its own Joyalization.)

Remark 1.5
.

The components of
prod
are morphisms of the form

\text{prod}_{F}:\textsf{p}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes\textsf{p}[S_{k}]\to\textsf{p}[F].

As we shall see,
\textsf{p}[F]
is often some intractable, ambient object, and the morphism
\text{prod}_{F}
is picking out those ‘semisimple’ elements which factorize.

Example 1.5
.

The
Cat
-valued species of compositions
\mathsf{\Sigma}
is Joyal-theoretic since

\mathsf{\Sigma}=\textsf{Br}(\Sigma).

In particular,

\mathsf{\Sigma}[F]=\Sigma[S_{1}]\times\dots\times\Sigma[S_{k}].

A morphism
\eta:\textsf{p}\to\textsf{q}
of Joyal-theoretic species is called
Joyal-theoretic
if it similarly factorizes, that is

\eta_{(\,)}=\text{id}_{1_{\textsf{C}}},\qquad\eta_{F}=\eta_{(S_{1})}\otimes\dots\otimes\eta_{(S_{k})},

\sigma_{F}=\sigma_{(S_{1})}\otimes\dots\otimes\sigma_{(S_{k})},\qquad\beta_{F}=\text{id}.

If
\eta
is Joyal-theoretic, then to check coherence it is enough to check
\sigma
-coherence in the totally lumped case
F=(I)
only. We show this in the case
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Cat}
at the level of components of the
2
-cells, with the general case following similarly. For
\sigma
-coherence, given
\mathtt{a}=(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})\in\textsf{p}[F]
, we have

{{(\sigma\circ\tau)}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}=\Big(\bigtimes_{1\leq i\leq k}{{(\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}}\circ\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}})}}_{\mathtt{a}_{i}}\Big)\circ\Big(\bigtimes_{1\leq i\leq k}(\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}})_{\eta_{(S_{i})}(\mathtt{a}_{i})}\Big)

=\underbrace{\bigtimes_{1\leq i\leq k}\Big({{(\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}}\circ\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}})}}_{\mathtt{a}_{i}}\circ(\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}})_{\eta_{(S_{i})}(\mathtt{a}_{i})}\Big)=\bigtimes_{1\leq i\leq k}\Big(\eta_{(S^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\big((\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}})_{\mathtt{a}_{i}}\big)\circ{\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}_{i}}\circ({\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}}}_{\mathtt{a}_{i}})^{\prime}\Big)}_{\text{by $\sigma$-coherence in the case $F=(I)$}}

=\Big(\bigtimes_{1\leq i\leq k}\eta_{(S^{\prime\prime}_{i})}\big((\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}},\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}})_{\mathtt{a}_{i}}\big)\Big)\circ\Big(\bigtimes_{1\leq i\leq k}{\tau|_{S^{\prime\prime}_{i}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}_{i}}\Big)\circ\Big(\bigtimes_{1\leq i\leq k}({\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{i}}}_{\mathtt{a}_{i}})^{\prime}\Big)=\eta_{F^{\prime\prime}}\big((\sigma,\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\tau}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ{\sigma}^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}.

Whereas
(\sigma,\beta)
-coherence and
\beta
-coherence are trivially satisfied.

A morphism
\eta:\textsf{p}\to\textsf{q}
of strongly Joyal-theoretic species is called
strongly
Joyal-theoretic
if the morphism

\text{prod}_{\textsf{q}}^{-1}\circ\eta\circ\text{prod}_{\textsf{p}}:\breve{\textsf{p}}\to\breve{\textsf{q}}

is Joyal-theoretic, i.e. if
\eta
is Joyal-theoretic up to specified isomorphism.

We leave implicit the constructions and definitions for cospecies which are analogous to those of this section.

2.
Bimonoids

2.1.
Bimonoids in Braid Arrangement Species

Given compositions
F=(S_{1},\dots,S_{k})
and
G=(T_{1},\dots,T_{l})
of the same finite set
I
, their
Tits product

FG
is the composition of
I
obtained by restricting
G
to each of the lumps of
F
, and then concatenating,

FG:=G|_{S_{1}};\,\cdots\,;G|_{S_{k}}.

This gives the set
\Sigma[I]
the structure of a monoid. The identity is
(I)
when
I\neq\emptyset
, and
(\,)
when
I=\emptyset
. The theory of bimonoids in braid arrangement species depends crucially on this monoidal structure. If
G\leq F
, then for a given permutation
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{l}
we let
\hat{\beta}\in\text{Sym}_{k}
such that

\hat{\beta}\circ F=\widetilde{G}F

where
\widetilde{G}=\beta\circ G
and
\widetilde{G}F
is the Tits product of
\widetilde{G}
with
F
(
\widetilde{G}F
and
F
do indeed have the same lumps because
G\leq F
).

Let
C
be a strict
2
-category. A
C
-valued (strong, strictly (co)unital)
bimonoid in species

h
is a
C
-valued species such that for each pair of compositions
F,G
with
G\leq F
, we have a pair of morphisms

\mu_{F,G}:\textsf{h}[F]\to\textsf{h}[G]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{F,G}:\textsf{h}[G]\to\textsf{h}[F]

called the multiplication and comultiplication respectively. We often abbreviate

(1)

\mu_{F}:=\mu_{F,(I)}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{F}:=\Delta_{F,(I)}.

We require an invertible
2
-cell
multiplication
\sigma
-naturality
and an invertible
2
-cell
multiplication
\beta
-naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[\widetilde{G}F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[\widetilde{G}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G,\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G,\beta}}

an invertible
2
-cell
associativity
and a trivial
2
-cell
strict unitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[E]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{G,E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,E}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G,E}}

an invertible
2
-cell
comultiplication
\sigma
-naturality
and an invertible
2
-cell
comultiplication

\beta
-naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[\widetilde{G}F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[\widetilde{G}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G,\sigma}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G,\beta}}

an invertible
2
-cell
coassociativity
and a trivial
2
-cell
strict counitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[E]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{G,E}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,E}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G,E}}

and an invertible
2
-cell
bimonoid axiom

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{h}[FG]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{h}[GF]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{G,A}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{FG,F}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{{GF},G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{B}_{F,G,A}}

for all meaningful choices, with the requirement that
F\neq G
,
\sigma\neq\text{id}_{I}
,
\beta\neq\text{id}_{(k)}
in (co)multiplication naturality and (co)associativity, and
F,G\neq A
in the bimonoid axiom (but
F=G
is allowed).
11
11

11

as with species, we could allow these choices and then add the coherence conditions that the resulting
2
-cells are all trivial
Thus, we have a bimonoid axiom
2
-cell
\text{B}_{F,G,A}
for each choice of compositions
F,G,A
such that
A<F,G
. Notice
FG
and
GF
always have the same lumps. If all the
2
-cells are trivial, i.e. the above nine diagrams commute on the nose, then the bimonoid is called
strict
. If
C
is a
1
-category, then a
C
-valued bimonoid must be strict.

.

E

\mu_{F,G}(F)=G\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{F,G}(G)=F

respectively.

Note that e.g. associativity
\beta
-coherence is well-defined; we have
\widetilde{E}F=\widetilde{E}GF
because
G\leq F
. One can check similar results for the other
3
-cells involving permutations.

Remark 2.1
.

We do not prove any coherence theorems in this paper, and it is only a conjecture that we have all the coherence conditions we should want in the correct definition of a bimonoid valued in a
2
-category
. We leave the conjectural formalization of these bimonoids as bialgebras over a
2
-bimonad
to future work.

Let us make these coherence conditions explicit in the case
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, at the level of components of the
2
-cells. The
2
-cell
\mu_{F,G,\sigma}
consists of a natural isomorphism in the category
\textsf{h}[G^{\prime}]

{\mu_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\mu^{\prime}_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})\to\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[F]
. The
2
-cell
\mu_{F,G,\beta}
consists of a natural isomorphism in the category
\textsf{h}[\widetilde{G}]

{\mu_{F,G,\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\widetilde{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}\to\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[F]
. The
2
-cell
\mu_{F,G,E}
consists of a natural isomorphism in the category
\textsf{h}[E]

{\mu_{F,G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\mu_{G,E}\big(\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})\big)\to\mu_{F,E}(\mathtt{a})

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[F]
. Similarly for the
2
-cells
\Delta_{F,G,\sigma}
,
\Delta_{F,G,\beta}
and
\Delta_{F,G,E}
. The
2
-cell
\text{B}_{F,G,A}
consists of a natural isomorphism in the category
\textsf{h}[G]

{\text{B}_{F,G,A}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\mu_{GF,G}\big(\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}(\mathtt{a})}\big)\to\mu_{G,A}\big(\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})\big)

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[F]
. Then associativity
\sigma
-coherence says

{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime},E^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{G^{\prime},E^{\prime}}\big({\mu_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\mu_{G,E,\sigma}}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}={\mu_{F,E,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\mu^{\prime}_{F,G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}

associativity
\beta
-coherence says

{\mu_{\widetilde{E}F,\widetilde{E}G,\widetilde{E}}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\mu_{\widetilde{E}G,\widetilde{E}}\big({\mu_{F,G,\hat{\beta}}}_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\mu_{G,E,\beta}}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}={\mu_{F,E,\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\widetilde{\mu_{F,G,E}}}_{\mathtt{a}}

\sigma
-functoriality multiplication coherence says

\mu_{F^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime\prime}}\big((\sigma,\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ{\mu^{\prime}_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\mu_{F,G,\sigma\circ\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

\beta
-functoriality multiplication coherence says

\mu_{\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}F,\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}}\big((\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma})_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G},\gamma}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ{\widetilde{\mu_{F,G,\beta}}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\mu_{F,G,\gamma\circ\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{(\beta,\gamma)}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

coassociativity
\sigma
-coherence says

{\Delta_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime},E^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ\Delta_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}({\Delta_{G,E,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\Delta_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\Delta_{G,E}(\mathtt{a})}={\Delta_{F,E,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\Delta^{\prime}_{F,G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}

coassociativity
\beta
-coherence says

{\Delta_{\widetilde{E}F,\widetilde{E}G,\widetilde{E}}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\Delta_{\widetilde{E}F,\widetilde{E}G}({\Delta_{G,E,\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\Delta_{F,G,\beta}}_{\Delta_{G,E}(\mathtt{a})}={\Delta_{F,E,\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\widetilde{\Delta_{F,G,E}}}_{\mathtt{a}}

\sigma
-functoriality comultiplication coherence says

\Delta_{F^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime\prime}}\big((\sigma,\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\Delta_{F^{\prime}G^{\prime},\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ{\Delta^{\prime}_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\Delta_{F,G,\sigma\circ\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

\beta
-functoriality comultiplication coherence says

\Delta_{\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}F,\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}}\big((\beta,\gamma)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ{\Delta_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G},\gamma}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ{\widetilde{\Delta_{F,G,\beta}}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\Delta_{F,G,\gamma\circ\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{(\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma})}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

associativity coherence says

{\mu_{F,E,D}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\mu_{E,D}({{\mu_{F,G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}})={\mu_{F,G,D}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\mu_{G,E,D}}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

coassociativity coherence says

{\Delta_{F,E,D}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{{\Delta_{F,G,E}}_{\Delta_{E,D}(\mathtt{a})}}={\Delta_{F,G,D}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\Delta_{F,G}({\Delta_{G,E,D}}_{\mathtt{a}})

and bimonoid
\sigma
-coherence says

\Delta_{G,A}({\mu_{F,A,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\Delta_{G,A,\sigma}}_{\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})}\circ{\text{B}^{\prime}_{F,G,A}}_{\mathtt{a}}

={\text{B}_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime},A^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{GF,G}({\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F,\sigma}}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ\mu_{GF,G}\big({(\sigma,\beta)}^{-1}_{\Delta_{FG,F}(\mathtt{a})}\big)\circ{\mu_{GF,G,\sigma}}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}(\mathtt{a})}}.

A
C
-valued
bimonoid in cospecies

h
is a
C
-valued cospecies
h
equipped with analogous data, requiring the analogous
3
-cells to commute. The functoriality coherence conditions have to be adjusted as follows;
\sigma
-functoriality multiplication coherence now says

\mu_{F^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime\prime}}\big((\sigma,\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ\underbrace{{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime},\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ{\mu^{\prime}_{F,G,\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{\text{$\sigma$ and $\tau$ switched}}={\mu_{F,G,\sigma\circ\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

\beta
-functoriality multiplication coherence now says

\mu_{\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}F,\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}}\big((\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma})_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\underbrace{\circ{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G},\beta}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ{\widetilde{\mu_{F,G,\gamma}}}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{\text{$\beta$ and $\gamma$ switched}}={\mu_{F,G,\gamma\circ\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{(\beta,\gamma)}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

\sigma
-functoriality comultiplication coherence now says

\Delta_{F^{\prime\prime},G^{\prime\prime}}\big((\sigma,\tau)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ\underbrace{{\Delta_{F^{\prime}G^{\prime},\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ{\Delta^{\prime}_{F,G,\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{\text{$\sigma$ and $\tau$ switched}}={\Delta_{F,G,\sigma\circ\tau}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ(\sigma,\tau)_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}

and
\beta
-functoriality comultiplication coherence now says

\Delta_{\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}F,\widetilde{\widetilde{G}}}\big((\beta,\gamma)_{\mathtt{a}}\big)\circ\underbrace{{\Delta_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G},\beta}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ{\widetilde{\Delta_{F,G,\gamma}}}_{\mathtt{a}}}_{\text{$\beta$ and $\gamma$ switched}}={\Delta_{F,G,\gamma\circ\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{(\hat{\beta},\hat{\gamma})}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}.

All the other coherence conditions remain unchanged.

2.2.
Lax Homomorphisms of Bimonoids

Let
C
be a strict
2
-category. Let
h
and
x
be bimonoids in
C
-valued species. A
lax homomorphism
, or just
homomorphism
, of bimonoids

\varphi:\textsf{h}\to\textsf{x}

consists of a morphism of the underlying species
\varphi=(\varphi_{F},{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}},\beta_{\mathtt{a}})
together with a pair of
2
-cells called
preservation of multiplication
and
preservation of comultiplication

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{x}[F]}}

for all meaningful choices. We have a well-defined composition of homomorphisms by composing
2

, respectively
