, if all the
2
-cells
{\sigma_{F}}
,
{\beta_{F}}
,
{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}
,
{\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,G}}
, are isomorphisms, respectively identities.

Let us make these coherence conditions explicit in the case
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, at the level of components of the
2
-cells. We have that
{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}
consists of a natural morphism in
\textsf{x}[G]

{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\mu_{F,G}\big(\varphi_{F}(\mathtt{a})\big)\to\varphi_{G}\big(\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})\big)

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[F]
, and
{\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,G}}
consists of a natural morphism in
\textsf{x}[F]

{\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\Delta_{F,G}\big(\varphi_{G}(\mathtt{a})\big)\to\varphi_{F}\big(\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})\big)

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[G]
. Then multiplication
\sigma
-naturality
coherence says

\varphi_{G^{\prime}}({\mu_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\sigma}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}\circ{\mathtt{\Phi}^{\prime}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\mathtt{\Phi}_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}({\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\mu_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\varphi_{F}(\mathtt{a})}

multiplication
\beta
-naturality coherence says

\varphi_{\widetilde{G}}({\mu_{F,G,\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\beta}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}\circ\widetilde{{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\mathtt{\Phi}_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}({\hat{\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}){\circ\mu_{F,G,\beta}}_{\varphi_{F}(\mathtt{a})}

associativity coherence says

\varphi_{E}({\mu_{F,G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\mathtt{\Phi}_{G,E}}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}\circ\mu_{G,E}({\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}})={\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\mu_{F,G,E}}_{\varphi_{F}(\mathtt{a})}

comultiplication
\sigma
-naturality coherence says

\varphi_{F^{\prime}}({\Delta_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\sigma}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}\circ{\mathtt{\Psi}^{\prime}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\mathtt{\Psi}_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}\circ\Delta_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}({\sigma_{G}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\Delta_{F,G,\sigma}}_{\varphi_{G}(\mathtt{a})}

comultiplication
\beta
-naturality coherence says

\varphi_{\widetilde{G}F}({\Delta_{F,G,\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\hat{\beta}}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}\circ\widetilde{{\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,G}}}_{\mathtt{a}}={\mathtt{\Psi}_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}\circ\Delta_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}({\beta}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\Delta_{F,G,\beta}}_{\varphi_{G}(\mathtt{a})}

coassociativity coherence says

\varphi_{F}({\Delta_{F,G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,G}}_{\Delta_{G,E}(\mathtt{a})}\circ\Delta_{F,G}({\mathtt{\Psi}_{G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}})={\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\mu_{F,G,E}}_{\varphi_{E}(\mathtt{a})}

and bimonoid coherence says

{\mathtt{\Psi}_{G,A}}_{\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})}\circ\Delta_{G,A}({\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,A}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\text{B}_{F,G,A}}_{\varphi_{F}(\mathtt{a})}

=\varphi_{G}({\text{B}_{F,G,A}}_{\mathtt{a}})\circ{\mathtt{\Phi}_{GF,G}}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}}(\mathtt{a})}\circ\mu_{GF,F}({\beta}_{\Delta_{FG,F}(\mathtt{a})})\circ\mu_{GF,G}(\widetilde{\mathtt{\Psi}_{FG,F}}_{\mathtt{a}}).

We leave the definition of lax homomorphisms for bimonoids in cospecies implicit. The coherence laws will all be unchanged.

2.3.
Joyal-Theoretic Bimonoids

Throughout this section, we assume all species and bimonoids are strict. One can develop the notion of Joyal-theoretic bimonoids in the non-strict setting, as we did for Joyal-theoretic species, however we shall not need it in this paper. Indeed, all the examples of bimonoids we come across for which the algebraic structure holds only up to coherent isomorphism are in any case not Joyal-theoretic.

Given a Joyal-theoretic species
h
, we can give
h
the structure of a bimonoid as follows. Let
h
be the connected Joyal species corresponding to
h
, so that
\textsf{h}=\textsf{Br}(\text{h})
. Suppose
h
is equipped with a pair of maps

(2)

\mu_{S,T}:\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]\to\textsf{h}[I]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{S,T}:\text{h}[I]\to\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]

for each decomposition
S\sqcup T=I
. When either
S
or
T
is the empty set, we require that
\mu_{S,T}
and
\Delta_{S,T}
are the unitors of
C
, e.g.
\mu_{\emptyset,T}
is the right unitor

\mu_{\emptyset,T}:\underbrace{\text{h}[\emptyset]}_{=1_{\textsf{C}}}\otimes\text{h}[T]\to\textsf{h}[T].

Therefore the actual data here is just a pair of maps
\mu_{S,T}
and
\Delta_{S,T}
for each two-lump composition
(S,T)
. Let
[I;2]
denote the set of two-lump compositions of
I
.

Remark 2.2
.

Without giving a full exposition of the (conjectural) generalized theory which uses decompositions in place of compositions, the inclusion of empty sets here may seem ad hoc. One can avoid them (since the data is just a pair of functions for each two-lump composition) but then needs to separately include several instances of the Joyal bimonoid axiom below, see
2.2
.

If
h
is to be a bimonoid, we need to define maps
\mu_{F,G}
and
\Delta_{F,G}
for compositions
G\leq F
. For
(S,T)\in[I;2]
, put

\mu_{(S,T),(I)}:=\mu_{S,T}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{(S,T),(I)}:=\Delta_{S,T}.

Towards defining
\mu_{F,G}
and
\Delta_{F,G}
in general, create a sequence of the form

G=F_{L}\leq\dots\leq F_{1}=F

where
F_{j-1}
is obtained from
F_{j}
by merging just two lumps, say
S_{j}
and
T_{j}
. This is always possible, but the sequence will not be unique. Put

\mu_{F_{j-1},F_{j}}:=\text{id}\otimes\dots\otimes\text{id}\otimes\mu_{S_{j},T_{j}}\otimes\text{id}\otimes\dots\otimes\text{id}

and

\Delta_{F_{j-1},F_{j}}:=\text{id}\otimes\dots\otimes\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{S_{j},T_{j}}\otimes\text{id}\otimes\dots\otimes\text{id}.

If we want
h
to satisfy associativity and coassociativity, we must then put

(3)

\mu_{F,G}:=\mu_{F_{L-1},F_{L}}\circ\dots\circ\mu_{F_{1},F_{2}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{F,G}:=\Delta_{F_{1},F_{2}}\circ\dots\circ\Delta_{F_{L-1},F_{L}}.

The Joyal associativity and Joyal coassociativity axioms, see below, ensure these definitions do not depend upon the choice of sequence
F_{1}\leq\dots\leq F_{L}
. Recalling the notation
(
1
)
(e.g.
\mu_{F}:=\mu_{F,(I)}
), and letting
G=(T_{1},\dots,T_{l})
, the would-be (co)multiplication factorizes as follows,

(4)

\mu_{F,G}=\mu_{F|_{T_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mu_{F|_{T_{l}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{F,G}=\Delta_{F|_{T_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\Delta_{F|_{T_{l}}}.

We now ask what properties must
\mu_{S,T}
and
\Delta_{S,T}
satisfy in order for
\mu_{F,G}
and
\Delta_{F,G}
to be the well-defined multiplication and comultiplication of a bimonoid. Consider the diagrams
Joyal multiplication naturality
and
Joyal comultiplication naturality
12
12

12

juxtaposition, e.g.
ST
or
\sigma\tau
, abbreviates disjoint union
S\sqcup T
or
\sigma\sqcup\tau

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[S^{\prime}]\otimes\text{h}[T^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[I]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[J]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S,T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[\sigma]\otimes\text{h}[\tau]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[\sigma\tau]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[S^{\prime}]\otimes\text{h}[T^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[I]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[J]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}}]\otimes\text{h}[\sigma|_{T^{\prime}}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[\sigma]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S,T}}

the diagrams
Joyal associativity
and
Joyal coassociativity

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]\otimes\text{h}[U]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[TU]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[ST]\otimes\text{h}[U]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[I]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S,T}\otimes\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes\mu_{T,U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S,TU}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{ST,U}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]\otimes\text{h}[U]}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[ST]\otimes\text{h}[U]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{h}[I]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S,T}\otimes\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes\Delta_{T,U}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S,TU}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{ST,U}}

and the diagram
Joyal bimonoid axiom

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[ST]\otimes\text{h}[UV]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[I]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[SU]\otimes\text{h}[TV]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]\otimes\text{h}[U]\otimes\text{h}[V]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[U]\otimes\text{h}[T]\otimes\text{h}[V]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S,T}\otimes\Delta_{U,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{ST,UV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{SU,TV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes B\otimes\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{S,U}\otimes\mu_{T,V}}

where
B
denotes the braiding
B:\textsf{h}[T]\otimes\textsf{h}[U]\xrightarrow{\sim}\textsf{h}[U]\otimes\textsf{h}[T]
. It is important we allow the sets
S,T,U,V
to be empty in these diagrams.

Example 2.2
.

If
U=\emptyset
and
S,T,V\neq\emptyset
, then the bimonoid axiom is equivalent to the following diagram consisting of only nonempty sets,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[I]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[ST]\otimes\text{h}[V]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[TV]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\text{h}[S]\otimes\text{h}[T]\otimes\text{h}[V]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S,T}\otimes\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{ST,V}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{S,TV}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\otimes\mu_{T,V}}

There are several other diagrams like this, obtained by setting various subsets equal to the empty set. If we did not allow
S,T=\emptyset
in
(
2
)
, then we would have had to separately include each instance of these diagrams in our list of axioms.

Remark 2.3
.

To relate this to more familiar notions of bimonoid or bialgebra, one can check that in the case
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Vec}
, these Joyal axioms say exactly that
\mu_{S,T}
and
\Delta_{S,T}
give
h
the structure of a (connected) bimonoid internal to Joyal vector species with respect to the Day convolution. Such an internal bimonoid decategorifies to give classical connected graded bialgebras in various ways, see
[
AM10
, Chapter 15]
.

A similar result holds for
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Set}
, but there the bimonoid is a bialgebra over a bimonad, rather than an internal bimonoid.

Theorem 2.1

(Aguiar-Mahajan)
.

The maps
\mu_{F,G}
and
\Delta_{F,G}
as defined in
(
3
)
give
h
the structure of a bimonoid if and only if the diagrams Joyal (co)multiplication naturality, Joyal (co)associativity and the Joyal bimonoid axiom commute.

Proof.

This result is one of the key ideas behind Aguiar-Mahajan’s generalization of species and bimonoids, see
[
AM13
, Proposition 50]
and
[
AM22
, Section 2.12.3]
. The author cannot find an explicit proof in their published work so far. The only non-trivial part of the proof is the bimonoid axiom, which can be proved using cartesian product notation or sumless Sweedler notation for the cases
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Set},\textsf{Vec},\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
. For a generic symmetric monoidal
1
-category

C
, the bimonoid axiom can be proved using string diagram notation in
C
, which we sketch below.

Let us deal with the ‘only if’ direction first. For
S,T\neq\emptyset
, Joyal (co)multiplication naturality is a special case of (co)multiplication
\sigma
-naturality, obtained by setting
F=(S,T)
. (Co)multiplication naturality is trivial if
S=\emptyset
or
T=\emptyset
. For
S,T,U\neq\emptyset
, Joyal associativity is obtained from the associativity diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}\big[(ST,U)\big]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}\big[(S,TU)\big]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}\big[(S,T,U)\big]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[I]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}\big[(S,T,U)\big)}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{h}[I]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(S,T,U),(I)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(S,T,U),(ST,U)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(ST,U),(I)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(S,T,U),(I)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(S,T,U),(S,TU)}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{(S,TU),(I)}}

by gluing along the morphism
\mu_{(S,T,U),(I)}
. Joyal associativity is trivial if any of
S,T,U
are empty. Joyal coassociativity follows similarly. The Joyal bimonoid axiom is either trivial, or a case of the bimonoid axiom where
l(F),l(G)\leq 2
. For example, if
S,T,U,V\neq\emptyset
, then we obtained the bimonoid axiom with
F=(ST,UV)
and
G=(SU,TV)
, and if
U=\emptyset
as in
2.2
, then we obtain the bimonoid axiom with
F=(ST,V)
and
G=(S,TV)
.

For the ‘if’ direction, there are nine diagrams to check. For
\sigma
-multiplication naturality, we have

\sigma\circ\mu_{F,G}=\Big(\bigotimes_{1\leq j\leq l}\sigma|_{T^{\prime}_{j}}\Big)\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{1\leq j\leq l}\mu_{F|_{T_{j}}}\Big)=\underbrace{\bigotimes_{1\leq j\leq l}\sigma|_{T^{\prime}_{j}}\circ\mu_{F|_{T_{j}}}=\bigotimes_{1\leq j\leq l}\Big(\mu_{F^{\prime}|_{T^{\prime}_{j}}}\circ\bigotimes_{\mathtt{i}_{j}}\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{\mathtt{i}_{j}}}\Big)}_{\text{Joyal multiplication naturality}}=\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}\circ\sigma.

Then
\sigma
-comultiplication naturality follows similarly from Joyal comultiplication naturality. We have that
\beta
-(co)multiplication naturality follows from the fact that the braiding of
C
is a natural transformation. (Co)associativity follows directly from the definition
(
3
)
, with Joyal (co)associativity ensuring that the (co)multiplication is well-defined.

To prove the bimonoid axiom, it is sufficient to consider the totally lumped case
A=(I)
, since the generic case
A=(A_{1},\dots,A_{a})
then follows by factorization of the (co)multiplication as follows,

\Delta_{G,A}\circ\mu_{F,A}=\Big(\bigotimes_{1\leq m\leq a}\Delta_{G|_{A_{m}}}\Big)\circ\Big(\bigotimes_{1\leq m\leq a}\mu_{F|_{A_{m}}}\Big)

=\underbrace{\bigotimes_{1\leq m\leq a}\big(\Delta_{G|_{A_{m}}}\circ\mu_{F|_{A_{m}}}\big)=\bigotimes_{1\leq m\leq a}\big(\mu_{G|_{A_{m}}F|_{A_{m}},G|_{A_{m}}}\circ\beta\circ\Delta_{F|_{A_{m}}G|_{A_{m}},F|_{A_{m}}}\big)}_{\text{bimonoid axiom in the case $A=(I)$}}=\mu_{GF,G}\circ\beta\circ\Delta_{FG,F}.

We use string diagram notation in
C
. Abbreviating
I=\textsf{h}[I]
,
\mu=\mu_{F}
and
\Delta=\Delta_{F}
, the multiplication of
h
is then denoted e.g.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{5}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{6}}}

where
F=(S_{1},\dots,S_{6})
and
G=(T_{1}=S_{1}\sqcup S_{2}\sqcup S_{3},T_{2}=S_{4}\sqcup S_{5},T_{3}=S_{6})
. Similarly for the comultiplication of
h
. The bimonoid axiom in the case
A=(I)
is then denoted e.g.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{5}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{Y_{6}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{=}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{2}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{3}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{4}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{5}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{X_{6}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{S_{2}}}

Drawn here is the diagram for the case
l(F)=3
and
l(G)=2
. In general,
F
has length
k
and
G
has length
l
, each

on the right-hand side divides into

many

’s, and each

is the union of

many

’s. Note some of the
X
’s and

’s may be empty, in which case one can either complete maps by tensoring with unitors, or just remove those nodes from the diagram.

The Joyal bimonoid axiom is denoted

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}}}

First, on the left-hand side of the bimonoid axiom string digram, use (co)assosiativity to replace the top and bottom nodes with the corresponding left comb trees (or indeed any appropriate binary tree). For example, if
l(F)=3
and
l(G)=4
, we obtain the string diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{1}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{T_{2}}}

All that appears now is binary (co)multiplication. Next, we repeatedly substitute in the right-hand side of the Joyal bimonoid axiom until no
\mu
comes before a
\Delta
. This is always possible, since the Joyal bimonoid axiom effectively permutes a
\mu
occurring before an adjacent
\Delta
, and replaces them with two
\Delta
’s occurring before two
\mu
’s.

For example, in the above string diagram, we have one pair of adjacent

and

in the wrong order, marked with a thick line. After permuting them using the Joyal bimonoid axiom, we obtain the string diagram

-naturality of

, we can move the

and

nodes around in the interior of the diagram arbitrarily, which we already look the liberty of doing to draw the last diagram. Therefore we can draw the diagram so that all braidings happen after the

’s, but before the

’s. For example, by moving nodes, the above diagram becomes

Finally, using (co)associativity, we replace consecutive
\Delta
nodes and
\mu
nodes with single
\Delta
’s and
\mu
’s, which yields the right-hand side of the bimonoid axiom. The actual braiding that happens in the interior of the diagram is arbitrary by the hexagon identities of
C
.
∎

The analogous construction for bimonoids in cospecies is left implicit. A
strictly Joyal-theoretic bimonoid
, abbreviated
Joyal-theoretic bimonoid
, is a strictly Joyal-theoretic (co)species
h
equipped with a bimonoid structure given by the construction above. Equivalently, it is a bimonoid whose (co)multiplication factorizes as follows,

\mu_{F,G}=\mu_{F|_{T_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\mu_{F|_{T_{l}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{F,G}=\Delta_{F|_{T_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\Delta_{F|_{T_{l}}}.

A
strongly Joyal-theoretic bimonoid
is a strongly Joyal-theoretic (co)species
h
equipped with a bimonoid structure which is Joyal-theoretic up to the specified isomorphism. That is, we have factorization

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{F,G}=\boldsymbol{\mu}_{F|_{T_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\boldsymbol{\mu}_{F|_{T_{l}}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{F,G}=\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{F|_{T_{1}}}\otimes\dots\otimes\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{F|_{T_{l}}}

where

\boldsymbol{\mu}_{F,G}=\text{prod}^{-1}_{G}\circ\mu_{F,G}\circ\text{prod}_{F},\qquad\boldsymbol{\Delta}_{F,G}=\text{prod}^{-1}_{F}\circ\Delta_{F,G}\circ\text{prod}_{G}

and
\mu_{F,G}
and
\Delta_{F,G}
are the (co)multiplication of
h
.

Example 2.3
.

The species of compositions
\mathsf{\Sigma}
is a Joyal-theoretic bimonoid, where
\mu_{S,T}
is given by concatenation and
\Delta_{S,T}
is given by restriction,

\mu_{S,T}(H,K):=H;K\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{S,T}(H)=(H|_{S},H|_{T}).

Notice that concatenation and restriction both preserve ‘
\leq
’. Thus
\mathsf{\Sigma}
has the structure of a bimonoid in
Cat
-valued species.

3.
Graded Species and Bimonoids

3.1.
Graded Braid Arrangement Species

Recall the category of categories
Cat
is cartesian, and the category of pointed categories
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
is equipped with the smash product of the cartesian product of categories, see e.g.
[
EM09
, Construction 4.19]
.

Let
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, and let
p
be a
C
-valued species (we do not assume that
p
is a strict species). A
p
-
graded set species

P
consists of a set
\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]
for each composition
F
and object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
, a function

\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[\sigma,F]:\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]\to\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]

for each bijection
\sigma:J\to I
, composition
F\in\Sigma[I]
and object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
, a function

\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F,\beta]:\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]\to\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{F}]

for each composition
F
, permutation
\beta\in\text{Sym}_{l(F)}
and object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
, and a function

\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{f}}[F,F]:\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]\to\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}[F]

for each composition
F
and morphism
\mathtt{a}\xrightarrow{\mathtt{f}}\mathtt{b}\in\textsf{p}[F]
. When there is no ambiguity, we abbreviate

(5)

\sigma:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[\sigma,F],\qquad\beta:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F,\beta],\qquad\mathtt{f}:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{f}}[F,F].

We require that the diagrams
graded
(\sigma,\beta)
-functoriality
,
(\sigma,\mathtt{f})
-functoriality
and
(\beta,\mathtt{f})
-functoriality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\widetilde{(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}\cong{\textsf{P}_{(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})^{\prime}}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\mathtt{b}}}[\widetilde{F}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tilde{\mathtt{f}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

the diagrams
graded
\sigma
-functoriality
and
graded
\sigma
-unitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})^{\prime}}[F^{\prime\prime}]}\cong{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime\prime}}[F^{\prime\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\circ\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{I}}

the diagrams
graded
\beta
-functoriality
and
graded
\beta
-unitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\widetilde{(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]}\cong{\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}}[\widetilde{\widetilde{F}}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\gamma\circ\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{(k)}}

and the diagrams
\mathtt{f}
-functoriality
and
\mathtt{f}
-unitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{c}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{g}\circ\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}_{\mathtt{a}}}

commute for all meaningful choices. If
p
is strict, the isomorphisms ‘
\cong
’ in these diagrams can be replaced by equalities ‘
=
’. Otherwise, these isomorphisms abbreviate the corresponding components of the invertible
2
-cells of
p
. For example, the fully explicit graded
(\sigma,\beta)
-functoriality
diagram is

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})^{\prime}}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\widetilde{(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}}}

Similarly, graded
\sigma
-functoriality and graded
\beta
-functoriality are completed by including components of the
2
-cells
(\sigma,\tau)
and
(\beta,\gamma)
respectively.

Remark 3.1
.

As with species,
p
-graded set species
P
may be formalized as functors. These functors have the form

\textsf{P}:\textstyle\int\displaystyle\textsf{p}\to\textsf{Set}.

where
\int\textsf{p}
is the Grothendieck construction of
p
(itself formalized as a functor).

Remark 3.2
.

Recall the exponential species
E
from
1.2
, which we can take to be
Cat
-valued
by replacing singletons with the trivial category with one object. An
E
-graded set species is the same thing as a set species. In this way,
p
-graded set species generalize set species.

Let
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, and let
p
and
x
be
C
-valued species. Let
X
be an
x
-graded set species. Given a lax morphism of species

\eta:\textsf{p}\to\textsf{x},\qquad\eta=(\eta_{F},{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}},{\beta}_{\mathtt{a}})

we have the
pullback

p
-graded set species
\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}
given by

\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]:=\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F],

\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}_{\mathtt{a}}[\sigma,F]:={\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[\sigma,F],\qquad\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}_{\mathtt{a}}[F,\beta]:={\beta}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F,\beta],\qquad\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}_{\mathtt{f}}[F,F]:=\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{f})}[F,F].

Here, e.g.
{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}}
abbreviates
\textsf{X}_{{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}}}[F,F]
, according to
(
5
)
. Functoriality of
\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}
then follows directly from functoriality of
X
and coherence of
\eta
. For example, graded
(\sigma,\beta)
-functoriality
of
\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}
is the outer nonagon of the digram (and denoting the isomorphisms ‘
\cong
’ explicitly)

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}}[\widetilde{F}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}[\widetilde{F}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\eta^{\prime}_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}^{\prime}}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\eta^{\prime}_{F}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{\eta^{\prime}_{F}(\mathtt{a})}}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}((\tilde{\mathtt{a}})^{\prime})}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}].}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}[F^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\widetilde{(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})})}[\widetilde{F}^{\prime}]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}({(\sigma,\beta)}_{\mathtt{a}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\sigma,\beta)_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}}}

The top-left pentagon commutes by graded
(\sigma,\beta)
-functoriality of
X
, the top-right square commutes by
(\sigma,\mathtt{f})
-functoriality of
X
, the bottom-right square commutes by
(\beta,\mathtt{f})
-functoriality of
X
, and the bottom-right hexagon commutes by
(\sigma,\beta)
-coherence of
\eta
. The other functoriality diagrams of
\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}
follow similarly. In
3.1
, we elevate this result to the level of bimonoids.

Continue to let
p
and
x
be
C
-valued species for
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, equipped with a lax morphism

\eta:\textsf{p}\to\textsf{x}.

Let
X
be an
x
-graded set species and let
P
be a
p
-graded set species. A
morphism

\xi:\textsf{P}\to\textsf{X}
of graded set species over
\eta
consists of a function

\xi_{(F,\mathtt{a})}:\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]\to\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F]

for each composition
F
and object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[F]
such that the diagrams

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F^{\prime}}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}[F^{\prime}]}\cong{\textsf{X}_{\eta^{\prime}_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{F}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\eta_{\widetilde{F}}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}[\widetilde{F}]}\cong{\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}}[\widetilde{F}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{(F,\mathtt{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{(F^{\prime},\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{(F,\mathtt{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{(\widetilde{F},\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{b}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\eta_{F}(\mathtt{b})}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\eta_{F}(\mathtt{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{(F,\mathtt{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\xi_{(F,\mathtt{b})}}

commute for all meaningful choices. Morphism composition is given by composing components. Note the composition of a morphism over
\eta_{1}
with a morphism over
\eta_{2}
is a morphism over
\eta_{2}\circ\eta_{1}
. This defines the category of graded set species.

Remark 3.3
.

In the formalization of graded species as functors, see
3.1
, the pullback
\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}
is given by
\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}=\textsf{X}\circ\int\eta
, and a morphism of graded species
\textsf{P}\to\textsf{X}
over
\eta
is the same thing as a natural transformation
\textsf{P}\Rightarrow\eta^{\ast}\textsf{X}
.

We also have
graded set cospecies
, the only differences being
p
is now a cospecies, and the source and target of
\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[\sigma,F]
and
\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[F,\beta]
are switched. Pullbacks and morphisms defined similarly. Finally, we have
p
-
graded

vector (co)species

P
, obtained by replacing sets/functions with vector spaces/linear maps above. Pullbacks and morphisms defined similarly to the set case.

3.2.
Bimonoids in Graded Braid Arrangement Species

Let
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, and let
h
be a bimonoid in
C
-valued species (we do not assume that
h
is a strict bimonoid). An
h
-graded bimonoid

H
is an
h
-graded set species such that for each pair of compositions
G\leq F
we have functions

{\mu_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]\to\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[G]\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\Delta_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[G]\to\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[F]

for each object
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[F]
and
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[G]
respectively, such that the diagrams
graded multiplication
\sigma
-naturality
and
graded multiplication
\beta
-naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{G}F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mu^{\prime}_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[G^{\prime}]}\cong{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}[G^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\widetilde{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[\widetilde{G}]}\cong{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}[\widetilde{G}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}

the diagram
multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{b})}[G]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{b}}}

the diagrams
graded comultiplication
\sigma
-naturality
and
graded comultiplication
\beta
-naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta^{\prime}_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[F^{\prime}]}\cong{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}}[\widetilde{G}F]\cong{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}[\widetilde{G}F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[G^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{G}]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G}}

the diagram
comultiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{b})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[G]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{F,G}(\mathtt{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{b}}}

the diagrams
graded associativity
and
graded unitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,E}(\mathtt{a})}[E]\cong\textsf{H}_{\mu_{G,E}(\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a}))}[E]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{G,E}}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,F}}_{\mathtt{a}}}

the diagrams
graded coassociativity
and
graded counitality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{G,E}(\mathtt{a})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F,G}(\Delta_{G,E}(\mathtt{a}))}[F]}\cong{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{F,E}(\mathtt{a})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[E]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{F,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{F,G}}_{\Delta_{G,E}(\mathtt{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{G,E}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
id
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{F,F}}_{\mathtt{a}}}

and the diagram
graded bimonoid axiom

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})}[A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{G,A}(\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a}))}[G]}\cong{\textsf{H}_{\mu_{GF,G}(\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}(\mathtt{a})})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\Delta_{FG,F}(\mathtt{a})}[FG]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}}(\mathtt{a})}[GF]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,A}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{G,A}}_{\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{FG,F}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{GF,G}}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}}(\mathtt{a})}}

commute for all meaningful choices. The isomorphisms ‘
\cong
’ abbreviate components of the invertible
2
-cells of
h
, similar to graded species.

Remark 3.4
.

Recall that
E
is a bimonoid, see
2.1
. An
E
-graded bimonoid is the same thing as a bimonoid in
Set
-valued species. In this way,
h
-graded bimonoids generalize bimonoids in
Set
-valued species.

An
h
-graded bialgebra

H
is defined analogously, by replacing sets/functions with vector spaces/linear maps above.

Theorem 3.1
.

Let
x
and
h
be bimonoids in
C
-valued species for
\textsf{C}\in\{\textsf{Cat},\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\}
, and let
X
be an
x
-graded bimonoid (respectively bialgebra). Given a lax homomorphism of bimonoids

\varphi:\textsf{h}\to\textsf{x},\qquad\varphi=(\varphi_{F},{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}},\beta_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}},{\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}})

the pullback
h
-graded set species (respectively vector species)
\textsf{H}:=\varphi^{\ast}\textsf{X}
is moreover an
h
-graded
bimonoid (respectively
h
-graded bialgebra), with multiplication and comultiplication given by

{\mu_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}:={\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\mu_{F,G}}_{\varphi(\mathtt{a})}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\Delta_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}:={\mathtt{\mathtt{\Psi}}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}\circ{\Delta_{F,G}}_{\varphi(\mathtt{a})}

respectively.

Proof.

We have eleven diagrams to check. Throughout this proof, we let

X_{(-)}=\varphi_{F}(-).

We can think of
X_{\mathtt{a}}
as the data which is indexed by the combinatorial object
\mathtt{a}
, and
{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}[F]}
is a set or vector space associated to
X_{\mathtt{a}}
. We also use the abbreviations ‘
\cong
’ for components of
2
-cells throughout this proof, in the hope that it makes the commutative diagrams easier to comprehend.

Graded
\sigma
-multiplication naturality of
H
is the outside octagon of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}}[F^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu^{\prime}_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}[G^{\prime}]}\cong{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}(X^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}})}[G^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}(X_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}})}[G^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X^{\prime}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[G^{\prime}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu^{\prime}_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[G^{\prime}]}\cong{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}(\mathtt{a}^{\prime})}}[G^{\prime}]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}({\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}^{\prime}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}_{X^{\prime}_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F^{\prime},G^{\prime}}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\sigma}_{\mathtt{a}}}

The top-left square commutes by graded multiplication
\sigma
-naturality of
X
, the top-right square commutes by multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
, the bottom-left square commutes by
(\sigma,\mathtt{f})
-functoriality
of
X
, and the bottom-right square commutes by multiplication
\sigma
-naturality coherence of
\varphi
. Thus, the outer octagon commutes.

Graded
\beta
-multiplication naturality of
H
is the outside octagon of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{X}_{\mathtt{a}}}[\widetilde{G}F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}}[\widetilde{G}F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}}[\widetilde{G}]\cong{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}(\widetilde{X}_{\mathtt{a}})}[\widetilde{G}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}(X_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}})}[\widetilde{G}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{X}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[\widetilde{G}]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\widetilde{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}}[\widetilde{G}]}\cong{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}(\tilde{\mathtt{a}})}}[\widetilde{G}]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}_{X_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}_{\tilde{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hat{\beta}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}}_{\widetilde{X}_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\widetilde{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\hat{\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\widetilde{G}F,\widetilde{G}}({\hat{\beta}}_{\mathtt{a}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}

The top-left square commutes by graded multiplication
\beta
-naturality of
X
, the top-right square commutes by multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
, the bottom-left square commutes by
(\beta,\mathtt{f})
-functoriality
of
X
, and the bottom-right square commutes by multiplication
\beta
-naturality coherence of
\varphi
. Thus, the outer octagon commutes. Graded
\sigma
-comultiplication naturality and graded
\beta
-comultiplication naturality of
H
follow similarly, from graded comultiplication naturality of
X
, comultiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
, functoriality of
X
, and comultiplication naturality coherence of
\varphi
.

Then multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
H
is the outside hexagon of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{b}}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{b}})}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{b})}}[G]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{f})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{f}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{X_{\mathtt{b}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}

The top square commutes by multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
, and the bottom square commutes by
\mathtt{f}
-functoriality of
X
and since
\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}
is a natural transformation. Thus, the outer hexagon commutes. Then comultiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
H
follows similarly, from comultiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
,
\mathtt{f}
-functoriality of
X
and since
\mathtt{\Psi}_{F,G}
is a natural transformation.

Graded associativity of
H
is the outside hexagon of the diagram

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{G,H}(X_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})})}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F,H}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}[F]}\cong{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{G,E}(\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}}))}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F,H}(\mathtt{a})}}[F]}\cong{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{G,E}(\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a}))}}[F]}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,H}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,G}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{G,H}}_{\mu_{F,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{G,H}}_{X_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{G,H}}_{\mu_{F,G}(\mathtt{a})}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{G,H}({\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,G}}_{\mathtt{a}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,H}}_{\mathtt{a}}}

The left triangle commutes by graded associativity of
X
. The right triangle commutes by associativity coherence of
\varphi
. The middle square commutes by multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
. Thus, the outer hexagon commutes. Then graded coassociativity of
H
follows similarly, from graded coassociativity of
X
, coassociativity coherence of
\varphi
, and comultiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
.

The graded bimonoid axiom of
H
is the outside decagon of the diagram
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})}}}[A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{F,A}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}}[A]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\Delta_{G,F}(X_{\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})})}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}[F]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{GF,G}(\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}}(X_{\mathtt{a}}))}}[G]}\cong{{\textsf{X}_{\Delta_{G,A}(\mu_{F,A}(X_{\mathtt{a}}))}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{X_{\Delta_{G,F}(\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a}))}}}[A]\cong{{\textsf{X}_{X_{\mu_{GF,G}(\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,F}(\mathtt{a})})}}}}[A]}.}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}}}[GF]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{GF,G}(\widetilde{{X}_{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a})}})}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\Delta_{FG,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}}[FG]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\mu_{GF,G}({X}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a}})})}}[G]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{{X}_{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a})}}}[FG]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{\widetilde{{X}_{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a})}}}}[GF]}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{{\textsf{X}_{{X}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a}})}}}[GF]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\beta}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{GF,G}}_{{X}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a}})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{GF,G}}_{\widetilde{X_{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a})}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{FG,F}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Psi}_{GF,F}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{F,A}}_{X_{\mathtt{a}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,A}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{G,A}}_{X_{\mu_{F,A}(\mathtt{a})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathtt{\Psi}_{G,A}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\Delta_{G,A}({\mathtt{\Phi}_{F,A}}_{\mathtt{a}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!\!\!\!\!{{\Delta_{G,A}}_{\mu_{F,A}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{GF,G}(\widetilde{\mathtt{\Psi}_{FG,F}}_{\mathtt{a}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{GF,G}({\beta}_{\mathtt{a}})}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{\mathtt{\Psi}_{FG,F}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\!\!\!\!{{\mu_{GF,G}}_{\widetilde{\Delta_{FG,G}(X_{\mathtt{a}})}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mu_{GF,G}}_{\widetilde{{X}_{\Delta_{FG,G}(\mathtt{a})}}}}

The top-left pentagon commutes by the graded bimonoid axiom of
X
. The bottom-left square commutes by
(\beta,\mathtt{f})
-functoriality of
X
. The top-middle square commutes by comultiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality
of
X
. The top-right pentagon commutes by bimonoid coherence of
\varphi
. The
bottom-middle
square and the bottom-right square commute by multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality of
X
. Thus, the outer decagon commutes.
∎

We leave implicit the constructions and definitions for cospecies which are analogous to those of this section.

3.3.
Joyal-Theoretic Graded Species and Bimonoids

Similar to
Section 2.3
, throughout this section we assume all species
p
and bimonoids
h
are strict. One can develop the notion of
Joyal-theoretic
graded species and bimonoids in the non-strict setting, however we shall not need it in this paper.

Let
P
be a
p
-graded set species. We abbreviate

\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[I]:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[(I)],\qquad\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[\sigma]:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}}[\sigma,(I)],\qquad\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{f}}[I]:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{f}}[(I),(I)].

At the level of objects, let us denote

\{1\}=1_{\textsf{Cat}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\{1,\bullet\}=1_{\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}}

where ‘
\bullet
’ is the distinguished object of
1_{\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}}
as usual. The
Joyalization

\breve{\textsf{P}}
of
P
is the
\breve{\textsf{p}}
-graded species given by

\breve{\textsf{P}}_{1}\big[(\,)\big]:=1_{\textsf{Set}},\qquad\big(\text{and}\quad\breve{\textsf{P}}_{\bullet}\big[(\,)\big]:=\emptyset\quad\text{if }\textsf{C}=\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\big),

\breve{\textsf{P}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}[F]:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}_{1}}[S_{1}]\times\dots\times\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}_{k}}[S_{k}],

\breve{\textsf{P}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}[\sigma,F]:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}_{1}}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{1}}]\times\dots\times\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{a}_{k}}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{k}}],\qquad\breve{\textsf{P}}_{(\mathtt{f}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{f}_{k})}[F,F]:=\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{f}_{1}}[S_{1}]\times\dots\times\textsf{P}_{\mathtt{f}_{k}}[S_{k}]

with the action of permutations
\beta
given by permuting cartesian product factors. For
p
a weakly Joyal-theoretic species, a
p
-graded species
P
is called
weakly Joyal-theoretic
if it is equipped with a morphism

\text{prod}:\breve{\textsf{P}}\to\textsf{P}

over
\text{prod}:\breve{\textsf{p}}\to\textsf{p}
such that
\text{prod}_{(I,\mathtt{a})}=\text{id}
for all
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{p}[I]
. If
prod
is an isomorphism, respectively identity, then
P
is called
strongly
, respectively
strictly
, Joyal-theoretic. We abbreviate strictly Joyal-theoretic to just
Joyal-theoretic
.

Let
H
be a Joyal-theoretic
h
-graded set species, where
h
is a Joyal-theoretic bimonoid. Recall
h
is equipped with functors
\mu_{S,T}
and
\Delta_{S,T}
for each decomposition
S\sqcup T=I
, which we often abbreviate

\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}:=\mu_{S,T}(\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b})\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\mathtt{a}|_{S},\mathtt{a}|_{T}):=\Delta_{S,T}(\mathtt{a}).

Example 3.1
.

If
h
is
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
-valued, then
\textsf{h}[\emptyset]=1_{\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}}=\{1,\bullet\}
. It follows from the monoidal structure of
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
that we always have

\mu_{\emptyset,\emptyset}(1,1)=1,\qquad\mu_{\emptyset,\emptyset}(1,\bullet)=\mu_{\emptyset,\emptyset}(\bullet,1)=\bullet,\qquad\mu_{\emptyset,\emptyset}(\bullet,\bullet)=\bullet\,.

More generally, since functors must preserve the distinguished object, we have

\mu_{S,T}(\mathtt{a},\bullet)=\mu_{S,T}(\bullet,\mathtt{b})=\bullet

for all objects
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[S]
,
\mathtt{b}\in\textsf{h}[T]
. Similarly, we always have

\bullet|_{S}=\bullet\qquad\text{and}\qquad\bullet|_{T}=\bullet.

We can give
H
the structure of an
h
-graded bimonoid as follows. Let
H
take values on the empty set as follows

\textsf{H}_{1}[\emptyset]:=1_{\textsf{Set}}\qquad\big(\text{and}\qquad\textsf{H}_{\bullet}[\emptyset]:=\emptyset\quad\text{if }\textsf{C}=\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\big).

For each decomposition
S\sqcup T=I
of a finite set
I
, suppose we have functions

{\mu_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}={\mu}_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}:\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[T]\to\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}}[I]\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a}}:\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[I]\to\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}|_{T}}[T]

where
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[S]
,
\mathtt{b}\in\textsf{h}[T]
and
\mathtt{a}\in\textsf{h}[I]
respectively. When
S
or
T
are empty, we require these functions be the unitors of
Set
(or the unique function
\emptyset\to\emptyset
if
\textsf{C}=\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
and
\mathtt{a}=\bullet
or
\mathtt{b}=\bullet
).

For
(S,T)\in[I;2]
, we now set

\mu_{(\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b})}:=\mu_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}\qquad\text{and}\qquad{\Delta_{(S,T),(I)}}_{\mathtt{a}}:={\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a}}.

Towards defining
{\mu_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}
and
{\Delta_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}
in general, create a sequence of compositions the form

G=F_{1}\leq\dots\leq F_{m}=F

where
F_{j-1}
is obtained from
F_{j}
by merging just two lumps, say
S_{j}
and
T_{j}
. Put

{\mu_{F_{j},F_{j-1}}}_{\mu_{F,F_{j}}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}:=\text{id}\times\dots\times\text{id}\times\mu_{\mathtt{b}_{j},\mathtt{c}_{j}}\times\text{id}\times\dots\times\text{id}

where
\mathtt{b}_{j}
and
\mathtt{c}_{j}
are the
S_{j}
and
T_{j}
components of
\mu_{F,F_{j}}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})
respectively. Also put

{\Delta_{F_{j},F_{j-1}}}_{\Delta_{F_{j-1},G}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}:=\text{id}\times\dots\times\text{id}\times{\Delta_{S_{j},T_{j}}}_{\mathtt{d}_{j}}\times\text{id}\times\dots\times\text{id}

where
\mathtt{d}_{j}
is the
S_{j}\sqcup T_{j}
component of
\Delta_{F_{j-1},G}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})
. If we want
H
to satisfy graded associativity and graded coassociativity, we must then put

{\mu_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}:=

(6)

{\mu_{F_{2},G}}_{\mu_{F_{,}F_{2}}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}\circ{\mu_{F_{3},F_{2}}}_{\mu_{F_{,}F_{3}}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}\circ\dots\circ{\mu_{F_{m-1},F_{m-2}}}_{\mu_{F,F_{m-1}}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}\circ{\mu_{F,F_{m-1}}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}

and

{\Delta_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}:=

(7)

{\Delta_{F,F_{m-1}}}_{\Delta_{F_{m-1},G}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}\circ{\Delta_{F_{m-1},F_{m-2}}}_{\Delta_{F_{m-2},G}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}\circ\dots\circ{\Delta_{F_{3},F_{2}}}_{\Delta_{F_{2},G}(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}\circ{\Delta_{F_{2},G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}.

We can now ask what properties must
\mu_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}
and
{\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a}}
satisfy in order for
{\mu_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}
and
{\Delta_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}
to be the well-defined multiplication and comultiplication of an
h
-graded bimonoid.

Consider the diagrams
graded Joyal multiplication

\sigma
-
naturality
and
Joyal multiplication

\mathtt{f}
-
naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[T]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[S^{\prime}]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}^{\prime}}[T^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}}[I]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}\mathtt{b}^{\prime}}[J]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\times\tau}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime},\mathtt{b}^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma\tau}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[T]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{c}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{d}}[T]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}}[I]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{c}\mathtt{d}}[I]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}\times\mathtt{g}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{c},\mathtt{d}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}\mathtt{g}}

the diagram
graded Joyal associativity

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[T]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{c}}[U]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}\mathtt{c}}[TU]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}}[ST]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{c}}[U]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}\mathtt{c}}[I]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}\times\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\times\mu_{\mathtt{b},\mathtt{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}\mathtt{c}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b},\mathtt{c}}}

the diagrams
graded Joyal comultiplication

\sigma
-
naturality
and
Joyal comultiplication

\mathtt{f}
-
naturality

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{T}}[T]\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}|_{S^{\prime}}}[S^{\prime}]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}|_{T^{\prime}}}[T^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[I]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}[J]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma|_{S^{\prime}}\times\sigma|_{T^{\prime}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\sigma}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{S^{\prime},T^{\prime}}}_{\mathtt{a}^{\prime}}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{T}}[T]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}|_{T}}[T]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[I]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[I]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}|_{S}\times\mathtt{f}|_{T}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathtt{f}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{b}}}

the diagram
graded Joyal coassociativity

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{(\mathtt{a}|_{ST})|_{T}}[T]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{U}}[U]\ }
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\ \textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{TU}}[TU]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{ST}}[ST]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{U}}[U]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[I]}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a}|_{ST}}\times\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\times{\Delta_{T,U}}_{\mathtt{a}|_{TU}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{S,TU}}_{\mathtt{a}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{ST,U}}_{\mathtt{a}}}

and the diagram
graded Joyal bimonoid axiom

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}}[ST]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}}[UV]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}}[I]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}|_{SU}}[SU]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}|_{TV}}[TV]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{T}}[T]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}|_{U}}[U]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}|_{V}}[V]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{S}}[S]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}|_{U}}[U]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{a}|_{T}}[T]\times\textsf{H}_{\mathtt{b}|_{V}}[V].}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{\mathtt{a},\mathtt{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{SU,TV}}_{\mathtt{a}\mathtt{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\Delta_{S,T}}_{\mathtt{a}}\times{\Delta_{U,V}}_{\mathtt{b}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\text{id}\times B\times\text{id}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mu_{{\mathtt{a}|}_{S},\mathtt{b}|_{U}}\times\mu_{\mathtt{a}|_{T},\mathtt{b}|_{V}}}

Theorem 3.2
.

The maps
{\mu_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}
and
{\Delta_{F,G}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{l})}
as defined in
(
6
)
and
(
7
)
give
H
the structure of an
h
-graded bimonoid if and only if the diagrams graded Joyal (co)multiplication
\sigma
-naturality, Joyal (co)multiplication
\mathtt{f}
-naturality, graded Joyal (co)associativity and the graded Joyal bimonoid axiom commute.

Proof.

The proof follows along the same lines as
2.1
.
∎

The
Joyalization

\breve{\textbf{{P}}}
of a
p
-graded vector species
P
is defined analogously,

\breve{\textbf{{P}}}_{1}\big[(\,)\big]:=\Bbbk,\qquad\big(\text{and}\quad\breve{\textbf{{P}}}_{\bullet}\big[(\,)\big]:=0\quad\text{if }\textsf{C}=\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}\big),

\breve{\textbf{{P}}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}[F]:=\textbf{{P}}_{\mathtt{a}_{1}}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes\textbf{{P}}_{\mathtt{a}_{k}}[S_{k}],

\breve{\textbf{{P}}}_{(\mathtt{a}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{a}_{k})}[\sigma,F]:=\textbf{{P}}_{\mathtt{a}_{1}}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{1}}]\otimes\dots\otimes\textbf{{P}}_{\mathtt{a}_{k}}[\sigma|_{S^{\prime}_{k}}],\qquad\breve{\textbf{{P}}}_{(\mathtt{f}_{1},\dots,\mathtt{f}_{k})}[F,F]:=\textbf{{P}}_{\mathtt{f}_{1}}[S_{1}]\otimes\dots\otimes\textbf{{P}}_{\mathtt{f}_{k}}[S_{k}].

The terms
weakly/strongly/strictly Joyal-theoretic
are also defined analogously for
p
-graded vector species. As before, strictly Joyal-theoretic is abbreviated
Joyal-theoretic
.

If
H
is a Joyal-theoretic
h
-graded vector species, where
h
is a Joyal-theoretic bimonoid, we can give
H
the structure of an
h
-graded bialgebra by replacing sets/functions above with vector spaces/linear maps. Indeed,
3.2
goes through unchanged.

If the algebraic structure of an
h
-graded bimonoid/bialgebra
H
is obtained according to the above construction, then
H
is called
strictly Joyal-theoretic
, abbreviated
Joyal-theoretic
. If
h
is strongly Joyal-theoretic, then an
h
-graded
bimonoid/bialgebra is called
strongly Joyal-theoretic
if it is Joyal-theoretic up to the specified isomorphism.

We leave implicit the constructions and definitions for cospecies which are analogous to those of this section.

Part II
Permutohedral Space

We now use the theory we built in
Part I
to study the bimonoid structure of the toric variety of the permutohedron, called permutohedral space.

4.
Preliminaries

4.1.
Preposets

Given a finite set
I
, a
preposet

p
of
I
is a reflexive and transitive relation
\geq_{p}
on
I
. Let
[2;I]
denote the set of ordered pairs
(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})
of distinct elements
\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2}\in I
,
\text{i}_{1}\neq\text{i}_{2}
. We identify
p
with the following subset of
[2;I]
,

(8)

p:=\big\{(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})\in[2;I]\ \big|\ \text{i}_{1}\geq_{p}\text{i}_{2}\big\}.

For preposets
p
and
q
of
I
, we write

(9)

q\leq p\qquad\text{if}\quad p\subseteq q.

For
p
a preposet of
I
and
S\subseteq I
, the
restriction

p|_{S}
is the preposet of
S
given by

(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})\in p|_{S}\qquad\text{if and only if}\qquad(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})\in p.

Recall
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
denotes the monoidal category of pointed categories, with monoidal product the smash product of the cartesian product. We have the Joyal-theoretic
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
-valued cospecies of
augmented preposets

\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
, given by

\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[I]:=\big\{p\ \big|\ \text{$p$ is a preposet of $I$}\big\}\sqcup\{\bullet\},

\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[\sigma](\bullet):=\bullet,\qquad\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[\sigma](p):=\Big\{\big(\sigma(\text{i}_{1}),\sigma(\text{i}_{2})\big)\in[2;I]\ \Big|\ \text{i}_{1}\geq_{p}\text{i}_{2}\Big\}.

We make the convention that
\bullet\leq p
for all preposets
p
of
I
, and turn
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[I]
into a category by including a single morphism
q\to p
whenever
p\leq q
. At the level of sets, we have

\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[F]=\underbrace{\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[S_{1}]\times\dots\times\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[S_{k}]}_{\text{smash product}}=\underbrace{\big(\text{O}[S_{1}]\times\dots\times\text{O}[S_{k}]\big)}_{\text{cartesian product}}\sqcup\,\{\bullet\}

where
\text{O}[S_{i}]
is the set of all preposets of
S_{i}
.

4.2.
Total Preposets and Compositions

Preposets may be viewed as a generalization of compositions as follows. We say a preposet is
total
if at least one of

(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})\in p\qquad\text{and}\qquad(\text{i}_{2},\text{i}_{1})\in p

is true for all
(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})\in[2;I]
. We have the subcospecies of total preposets
\mathsf{\Sigma}^{\ast}\subset\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
, given by

\mathsf{\Sigma}^{\ast}[I]:=\big\{p\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[I]\ \big|\ p\ \text{is a total preposet}\big\}.

(The distinguished element ‘
\bullet
’ is not contained in
\mathsf{\Sigma}^{\ast}
, so this is a subcospecies at the level of
Cat
-valued
cospecies.) Given a composition
F\in\mathsf{\Sigma}[I]
, when there is no ambiguity we also let
F
denote its corresponding total preposet
F\in\mathsf{\Sigma}^{\ast}[I]
given by

F:=\big\{(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})\in[2;I]\ \big|\ \text{`the lump containing $\text{i}_{1}$' }\geq\text{ `the lump containing $\text{i}_{2}$'}\big\}.

The function

\mathsf{\Sigma}[I]\to\mathsf{\Sigma}^{\ast}[I],\qquad F\mapsto F

is a bijection which preserves ‘
\leq
’. The set
13
13

13

where
(S,T)
denotes its corresponding total preposet, and
(S,T)\leq p
refers to the relation
(
9
)

(10)

\big\{(S,T)\in[I;2]\ \big|\ (S,T)\leq p\big\}

determines
p
, since it records all of
p
’s upward-closed subsets. Indeed, we have
(S,T)\leq p
if and only if
S
, respectively
T
, is an upward-closed, respectively downward-closed, subset of
I
.

Remark 4.1
.

Note the duality between
(
8
)
and
(
10
)
;
(
8
)
contains all the
2
-probes
2\hookrightarrow I
of
I
which are compatible with
p
, whereas
(
10
)
contains all the
2
-coprobes
I\twoheadrightarrow 2
of
I
which are compatible with
p
.

4.3.
The Bimonoid of Augmented Preposets

We now give augmented preposets
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
the structure of a
Joyal-theoretic
bimonoid. We saw in
Section 2.3
that this amounts to defining a pair of functors
\mu_{S,T}
and
\Delta_{S,T}
for each two-lump composition
(S,T)
, and then checking the five Joyal diagrams.

For
(S,T)\in[I;2]
and elements
p\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[S]
and
q\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[T]
, let

(p\,|\,q):=\begin{cases}\text{disjoint set union of $p$ and $q$}&\quad\text{if }p,q\neq\bullet\\
\bullet\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[I]&\quad\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

For
(S,T)\in[I;2]
and
p\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[I]
, let

p\talloblong_{S}:=\begin{cases}p|_{S}&\quad\text{if $(S,T)\leq p$}\\
\bullet\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[S]&\quad\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad\ \ \ \ \text{and}\ \ \ \ \qquad p\!\fatslash_{\,T}:=\begin{cases}p|_{T}&\quad\text{if $(S,T)\leq p$}\\
\bullet\in\textsf{O}_{\bullet}[T]&\quad\text{otherwise.}\end{cases}

Proposition 4.1
.

The
\textsf{Cat}_{\bullet}
-valued cospecies of augmented preposets
\textsf{O}_{\bullet}
has the structure of a Joyal-theoretic bimonoid, with multiplication and comultiplication given by

\mu_{S,T}(p,q):=(p\,|\,q)\qquad\text{and}\qquad\Delta_{S,T}(p):=(p\talloblong_{S},p\!\fatslash_{\,T})

respectively.

Proof.

We have five diagrams to check. Joyal multiplication naturality, Joyal comultiplication naturality, and Joyal associativity are immediate. Recall Joyal coassociativity says

\big((p\talloblong_{ST})\talloblong_{S},(p\talloblong_{ST})\!\fatslash_{\,T},p\!\!\fatslash_{\,U}\big)=\big(p\talloblong_{S},(p\!\fatslash_{\,TU})\talloblong_{T},(p\!\fatslash_{\,TU})\!\!\fatslash_{\,U}\big).

If
(S,T,U)\leq p
, then

\big((p\talloblong_{ST})\talloblong_{S},(p\talloblong_{ST})\!\fatslash_{\,T},p\!\!\fatslash_{\,U}\big)=\big(p|_{S},p|_{T},p|_{U}\big)=\big(p\talloblong_{S},(p\!\fatslash_{\,TU})\talloblong_{T},(p\!\fatslash_{\,TU})\!\!\fatslash_{\,U}\big).

We claim that if
(S,T,U)\nleq p
, then

\big((p\talloblong_{ST})\talloblong_{S},(p\talloblong_{ST})\!\fatslash_{\,T},p\!\!\fatslash_{\,U}\big)=\,\bullet\,=\big(p\talloblong_{S},(p\!\fatslash_{\,TU})\talloblong_{T},(p\!\fatslash_{\,TU})\!\!\fatslash_{\,U}\big).

To see this, first note that if
(S,T,U)\nleq p
, then at least one of
(\text{t},\text{s}),(\text{t},\text{u}),(\text{u},\text{s})\in p
is true for
\text{s}\in S
,
\text{t}\in T
,
\text{u}\in U
. We have

(\text{t},\text{s})\in p\qquad\implies\qquad(p\talloblong_{ST})\talloblong_{S}=\bullet\quad\text{and}\quad p\talloblong_{S}=\bullet.

We have

(\text{t},\text{u})\in p\qquad\implies\qquad p\!\fatslash_{\,U}=\bullet\quad\text{and}\quad(p\!\fatslash_{\,TU})\!\!\fatslash_{\,U}=\bullet.

We have

(\text{u},\text{s})\in p\qquad\implies\qquad p\!\fatslash_{\,U}=\bullet\quad\text{and}\quad p\talloblong_{S}=\bullet.

Recall the Joyal bimonoid axiom says

\big((p\,|\,q)\talloblong_{SU},(p\,|\,q)\!\!\!\fatslash_{\,TV}\big)\,=\,\big((p\!\talloblong_{S}|\,q\talloblong_{U}),(p\!\!\fatslash_{\,T}|\,q\!\fatslash_{\,V})\big).

We have

(SU,TV)\leq(p\,|\,q)\qquad\iff\qquad(S,T)\leq p\quad\text{and}\quad(U,V)\leq q.

Therefore, if
(SU,TV)\nleq(p\,|\,q)
then

(p\,|\,q)\talloblong_{SU}=\bullet\qquad\text{and}\qquad(p\!\talloblong_{S}|\,q\talloblong_{U})=\bullet

and if
(SU,TV)\leq(p\,|\,q)
then

(p\,|\,q)\talloblong_{SU}=(p\,|\,q)|_{SU}=\big(p|_{S}\big|\,q|_{U}\big)=(p\!\talloblong_{S}|\,q\talloblong_{U})

and

(p\,|\,q)\!\!\fatslash_{\,TV}=(p\,|\,q)|_{TV}=\big(p|_{T}\big|\,q|_{V}\big)=(p\!\!\fatslash_{\,T}|\,q\!\fatslash_{\,V}).

Finally, notice
\mu_{S,T}
and
\Delta_{S,T}
preserve ‘
\leq
’ and ‘
\bullet
’, and so do indeed define functors of pointed categories.
∎

For the higher comultiplication, we denote e.g.

(p\talloblong_{S},p\talloblong_{T},p\talloblong_{U}):=\Delta_{(S,T,U)}(p).

4.4.
Root Datum of
\textmd{SL}_{I}(\mathbb{C})

For each finite set
I
, we have the lattice
\mathbb{Z}^{I}
of
\mathbb{Z}
-valued functions on
I
,

\mathbb{Z}^{I}:=\big\{\text{functions}\ \lambda:I\to\mathbb{Z}\big\}.

For
S\subseteq I
, let
\lambda_{S}\in\mathbb{Z}^{I}
denote the characteristic function of
S
,

\lambda_{S}:I\to\mathbb{Z},\qquad i\mapsto\begin{cases}1&\quad\text{if}\quad\text{i}\in S\\
0&\quad\text{if}\quad\text{i}\notin S.\end{cases}

If we consider functions only up to translations of
\mathbb{Z}
, we obtain the quotient lattice

\text{N}^{I}:={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\big\{\text{functions}\ \lambda:I\to\mathbb{Z}\big\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{Z}$}\right.}=\mathbb{Z}^{I}/\mathbb{Z}\lambda_{I}.

The lattice
\text{N}^{I}
is called the
weight lattice
of (the Lie algebra of)
\text{SL}_{I}(\mathbb{C})
. We have the Joyal-theoretic species
I\mapsto\text{N}^{I}
with the action of bijections given by
\sigma(\lambda):=\lambda\circ\sigma
.

We also have the lattice
\mathbb{Z}I
of formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations of elements of
I
,

\mathbb{Z}I:=\big\{h\ \big|\ h=(h_{\text{i}})_{\text{i}\in I},\,h_{\text{i}}\in\mathbb{Z}\big\}.

Then
\mathbb{Z}I
is the dual lattice of
\mathbb{Z}^{I}
via the perfect pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:\mathbb{Z}I\times\mathbb{Z}^{I}\to\mathbb{Z},\qquad\langle h,\lambda\rangle:=\sum_{\text{i}\in I}h_{\text{i}}\,\lambda(\text{i}).

Since
\text{N}^{I}
is a quotient of
\mathbb{Z}^{I}
, its dual lattice
\text{M}_{I}=\operatorname{Hom}(\text{N}^{I},\mathbb{Z})
is naturally a sublattice of
\mathbb{Z}I
, consisting of those formal
\mathbb{Z}
-linear combinations whose coefficients sum to zero

\text{M}_{I}:=\big\{h\in\mathbb{Z}I\ \big|\ \langle h,\lambda_{I}\rangle=0\big\}.

The lattice
\text{M}_{I}
is called the
coweight lattice
of
\text{SL}_{I}(\mathbb{C})
. We have the Joyal-theoretic cospecies
I\mapsto\text{M}^{I}
with the action of bijections given by

\langle\sigma(h),\lambda\rangle:=\langle h,\sigma(\lambda)\rangle,

i.e.
(\sigma(h))_{\text{i}}=h_{\sigma^{-1}(\text{i})}
for
\text{i}\in I
. We abbreviate
h^{\prime}:=\sigma(h)
as usual.

For
(S,T)\in[I;2]
, the
fundamental weight

\lambda_{ST}\in\text{N}^{I}
is defined to be the image of
\lambda_{S}
in
\text{N}^{I}
. We have
\lambda_{TS}=-\lambda_{ST}
. For
(\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2})\in[2;I]
, the
coroot

h_{\text{i}_{1}\text{i}_{2}}\in\text{M}_{I}
is given by

\langle h_{\text{i}_{1}\text{i}_{2}},\lambda\rangle:=\lambda(\text{i}_{1})-\lambda(\text{i}_{2}).

We have
h_{\text{i}_{2}\text{i}_{1}}=-h_{\text{i}_{1}\text{i}_{2}}
.

Remark 4.2
.

We have the following interesting isomorphisms. Given a cyclic ordering of
I
and
\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2}\in I
, let
[\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2}]\subseteq I
denote the interval from
\text{i}_{1}
inclusive to
\text{i}_{2}
exclusive. Then, for each cyclic ordering of
I
, we have the isomorphism given by

\text{M}_{I}\to\text{N}^{I},\qquad h_{\text{i}_{1}\text{i}_{2}}\mapsto\lambda_{[\text{i}_{1},\text{i}_{2}][\text{i}_{2},\text{i}_{1}]}.

These isomorphisms underlie objects such as alcove polytopes
[
LP07
]
and blades
[
Ear20
]
.

4.5.
Algebraic Tori

Let
\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I}
denote the diagonal maximal torus of
\text{PGL}_{I}(\mathbb{C})
, given by

\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I}:=(\mathbb{C}^{\times})^{I}/\mathbb{C}^{\times}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\{\text{functions}\ x:I\to\mathbb{C}^{\times}\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{C}^{\times}$}\right.}\cong\text{N}^{I}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

Let
\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}
denote the diagonal maximal torus of
\text{SL}_{I}(\mathbb{C})
, given by

\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}:=\Big\{y\ \Big|\ y=(y_{i})_{\text{i}\in I}\in\mathbb{C}^{\times}I,\ \prod_{\text{i}\in I}y_{\text{i}}=1\Big\}\cong\text{M}_{I}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{C}^{\times}.

We have the following natural identifications of (co)character lattices,

\text{N}^{I}=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\times},\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{\vee}_{I},\mathbb{C}^{\times})\qquad\text{and}\qquad\text{M}_{I}=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I},\mathbb{C}^{\times})=\operatorname{Hom}(\mathbb{C}^{\times},\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}).

In particular, for each
h\in\text{M}_{I}
we have the monomial

f^{h}:\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I}\to\mathbb{C},\qquad x\mapsto\prod_{\text{i}\in I}x(\text{i})^{h_{\text{i}}}

and for each
\lambda\in\text{N}^{I}
we have the monomial

f^{\lambda}:\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}\to\mathbb{C},\qquad y\mapsto\prod_{\text{i}\in I}y^{\lambda(\text{i})}_{\text{i}}.

Remark 4.3
.

We may interpret
\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I}
as the ‘worldsheet’
\mathbb{C}^{\times}
with
I
-marked points, modulo scaling. Complex permutohedral space will be a compactification of
\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I}
with boundary divisors in
one-to-one
correspondence with elements
(S,T)\in[I;2]
, which are approached by moving the points of
S
to the infinite future, or the points of
T
to the infinite past. This interpretation is suggested by the role the bimonoid of compositions
\mathsf{\Sigma}
plays in casual perturbation theory, see
[
Nor20
]
.

Recall one can also do algebraic geometry over the
tropical rig

\mathbb{T}:=\mathbb{R}\sqcup\{-\infty\}.

This is the rig with addition given by maximum and multiplication given by addition. Thus,
\mathbb{T}^{\times}=\mathbb{R}
. We have the following tropical analogs of the tori
\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I}
and
\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}
. Let
\text{T}^{I}=\mathbb{T}\text{T}^{I}
denote the tropical algebraic torus given by

\text{T}^{I}:=(\mathbb{T}^{\times})^{I}/\mathbb{T}^{\times}=\mathbb{R}^{I}/\mathbb{R}\lambda_{I}={\raisebox{1.99997pt}{$\{\text{functions}\ \lambda:I\to\mathbb{R}\}$}\left/\raisebox{-1.99997pt}{$\mathbb{R}$}\right.}\cong\text{N}^{I}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{T}^{\times}.

Let
\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}=\mathbb{T}\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}
denote the tropical algebraic torus given by

\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}:=\bigg\{h\ \bigg|\ h=(h_{\text{i}})_{\text{i}\in I}\in\mathbb{R}I,\ \sum_{\text{i}\in I}h_{\text{i}}=0\bigg\}\cong\text{M}_{I}\otimes_{\mathbb{Z}}\mathbb{T}^{\times}.

This is the sum-zero hyperplane of
\mathbb{R}I
. Similar to
\mathbb{C}\text{T}^{I}
, we may interpret
\text{T}^{I}
as the worldline
\mathbb{R}
with
I
-marked points, modulo
time-translational
symmetry.

At the level of real vector spaces, we have the identification
\text{T}^{I}=\operatorname{Hom}(\text{T}^{\vee}_{I},\mathbb{R})
given by the pairing

\langle-,-\rangle:\text{T}^{\vee}_{I}\times\text{T}^{I}\to\mathbb{R},\qquad\langle h,\lambda\rangle:=\sum_{\text{i}\in I}h_{\text{i}}\,\lambda(\text{i}).

In particular, for each
h\in\text{M}_{I}
we have the tropical monomial
